About Sunshades and En-tont-cese—Sunshades taken as a whole are more ornamental than useful, especially the transparent varieties which can do very little for us in the way of warding off the sun's rays. For real Comfort'a dark green silk sunshade lined with dark green chiffon is perhaps best, but this of course is not smart enough for the generality of women. White, cream, mole-colour, and bright red are the popular choice, bright red en-tout-ens being widely chosen for river, *eatable and country wear. No doubt the bright red sunshade, or tout-en-cas, is particularly effective, but alas, the glare from it is often worse than that of the sun itself. ! Shantung Silk.—Wben purchasing this particular ' fabric, it is well to make sure it is the real thing, which hails from Northern China, and not the imitation variety that comes from Francs. The former wears very much better than the latter, which is being sent over in such vast quantities and exhibited in our shop windows and shoxroome. 'Yet another step downwards is Shantung: ette, a cheap imitation of the French imitation, and one we should do well to pass by. MADGE.

AN IDEAL HOLIDAY IN CANADA. Under tho title of the " Royal Visit to Canada," the Allan Line have just issued a handy guide to the visit of His Royal Highness the Prince of Wales to Quebec in July. The brochure, after giving a concise account the story of Quebec refers in detail to the Quebec tercentenary celebrations and includes the oflicial programme. Space is also given to an account of the turbine floating palaces and the other stearGera of the Allan Line, together with a time table of sailings to and from Canada, cod particulars, with fares, cf delightful iuland tours. It may be noted that the round trip by a turbine steamer occupies three weeks, allowing about siN days ashore, and may be commended as an ideal holiday,

PHOTOCRAPHIC NOTES.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASIHON-HOIVIE.

LONG SLEEVES AND LACE GLOVES.

It is good to note that the long sleeve is being universally adopted for day wear, and. the short elbow manche, which entailed such prodigality in the matter of tong --gloves,- and which drove the girl on a small dress allowance to the brink of detair, is being almost uniVersally discarded, says a writer in the Ercning Standard. Num- bers of the new sleeves are made entirely of gauged transparent net: the fulness terminating in a little gathered frill, which runs down the outside seam from the .shoulder to the wrist, and the woman who is conscious of angularity in this respect is not likely to discard the chance of covering up her imperfection in u manner at once smart and becoming. Tight lace or muslin sleeves tucked all the way down and fitting over the lower part of the hand are equally in favour, while the cool . and simple method of wearing long lace gloves which is being adopted in Paris for young girls with both enthusiasm and suc- cess might well be imitated on this side of the Channel- where the sleeve cf medium length is concerned.

===== 0002601_19080613_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
A PRETTY DT:ST CLOAK.

A charming and most useful dust - coat is lured in the sketch--just the thing for wear r a pretty dress in dusty weather, for travel- r. or, in warm weather, for motoring. The tonal used iu its realisation is a good quality llienne in a light and very pretty shade of mit. This coat is quite of the loose-fitting

plat ovei ling mat sicil

type, being very slightly shaped into a, short waist-line just beneath the arms. From the neck turn back small revers and collar faced with biscuit silk, patterned with an excellent design in d,)lightful tones of blue and green. The coat fastens down the front in single- breasted fashion with four large mother-o'-pearl buttons. The sleeves are of considerable width, and are finished at the wrist by turn-back cuffs to match the collar. WHERE ARE OUR WAISTS?

It is not at all smart to shew the waist ip the new frocks of this season, says a Globe fashion writer. Both gowns and coats are made to fit with exactitude upon the shoulders, and to define the arms with something like closeness of fit as far as the elbows. At the back both sown and coat- are carried away from the figure by the skill of the tailor or modiste, and anyone who clings to the old-fashioned long waist, with the desire of shewing the result of so much tight. lacing and consequent pain and discomfort, must be disappointed to find that she is entirely out- side the pale of the fashions of the moment.- Still, there are many who do cling to the long- waisted gowns of a season or two since. Un- fortunately; they have a very dumpy and dowdy appearance as compared with 'the Empire and the Directoire modes. They are clothed, not dressed. Even the princess gown j,4 now made Empire at the back, either fully. so, or merely by catching up the skirt some two inches or so above the natural line of the waist. RILBON FOR HEADDRESSES.

Bands of ribbon, silk, or metal are often bound round the head, like the classic filet. Drooping marabout and osprey plumes are likely to be very much worn for come time, says the Quren. We have adopted the Greek typo of headdress for evening wear, or the Charles 11., two diametrically opposite styles. The ribbon is often run in and out through the hair with great effect; when the hair is light and fluffy this has to' be done carefully.

===== 0002601_19080613_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
A DAINTY BLOrSE.

Pictured in our illustration is a dainty summer blouse of rather unusual and particularly becom- ing shape. ,Fine white lawn is the material in which this pretty garment is realised, its admir- able washing properties making it particularly suitable for everyday wear. This blouse is ar- ranged in a succession of small tucks right across the shoulders both back and front, the tucks

being released just below the level of the arm- hole, and the resultant fulness allowed to pouch a little at the waist. From the neck, which is cut out in V-shape, turns back a collar of the same white lawn, edged with an embroidered scallop, and worked with a pretty and simple de- sign. A big sailor knot of pale green silk comes from beneath the collar, adding a touch of colour to the blouse. Embroidered cuffs to match the collar finish the simple three-quarter lecves, and the blouse is completed by a dainty chemisette of Valenciennes lace. SIMPLE SHIRTS OF NET.

Perfectly charming, declares a Tager writer, are the plain point d'esprit shirts posed on chiffon for wearing with the severe satin birec- toire coati which have received such an enthusi- astic welcome from the vraie elegante; they have a distinctive cachet, and shew to great ad- vantage the elaborate lace jabots so Popular this season. A touch of colour can be introduced by picking out the pattern of the lace of the cravat with floss cr cable silk of the same tone as the costume, while diminutiVe bows of black and coloured velvet with or without gold-fringed ends are a decidedly novel embellishment for these fashionable little dress accessories.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

SPINACH OUELET.—Make a puree of spinach in the usual way; that is to say, after having boiled it till tender, chop it very fine, and rub it through a coarse wire sieve, season with salt and pepper, stir over the fire and add two ounces of butter and a little cream. Take two table- spoonfuls of the spinach and stir it into four eggs which have been previou,ly beaten, yolks ana wnites separately; aca a Little piece ox shallot which has been rubbed through the sieve, and salt and pepper to taste. When thfiroughly mixed, put the whole into an omelet. pan, with two ounces of butter, and fry a pale brown. :Serve very hot.

ASBESTOS WOOD.

The asbestos wood invented by Professor C. L. Norton, and described in the Electrical World, should prove a useful substance. It .is absolutely; fire-proof, and of a highly in- sulating nature. In this latter quality it al- moat' equals mica, one of the most perfect in- sulators 'mown. Manufactured presumably from the mineral asbestos, this "asbestos wood" can be .werked with the ordinary wood-working tools, and can be sawn, nailed, or screwed, the same rs hardwood. It has a smooth surface, which will take any kind of finish. For . electrical work it is usually finished to look like ebony. It is used for ' Switch boards, switch bases, insulating pieces, &c. AL.° for the doors in front Of high voltage stations.

And he thinks so still, although he is away in South Africa fighting for his Queen. And Doris, poor little soul, is very sad in his absence and very anxious, too. She does not go out and enjoy her- self now he is away and in danger, as some of the forlorn wives do, but has gone back to live quietly with her grandparents in the dull house in Cairo- square, and there he will find her waiting for him when he conies home, if it please God that he does come home,

And Doris is not so very unhappy either, because she has her consolations. There is the six months' r.i.d baby to begin with, who is said to be Jack's living image, and then there are Jack's letters. One of them lies next her heart to-day as she croons over the babe ; and there is just one little sentence in it that brought the tears of joy into her eyes as she read it. "I always think," wrote Jack, from far away Pretoria, "that the very greatest piece of luck I ever had in my life was that day on the river whicl, first brought -you to me, darling!" After two years of matrimony such words 55 then are very good reading.

COCKROACHES IN AMBER.

Some of the finest fossil insects known to geologists are these preserved in amber. Originally a gum exuding from a species of pine tree, the amber first captured the insect, and then/ buried it, thus preserving every detail of its structure. The exhibition of a fine collection- of cockroaches thus preserved at a meeting of - the Entomological Society by I,lr. Shelford shews the respectable •anti- gully of this unpleasant insect. For " the amber depesits Of Eastern Prussia, from whence they Were obtained,. are of middle Tertiary age. The cockroach, as a matter of fact, however, can claim a much greater age, for a species of Blatta is recorded from the Purbeck beds. A related genus Palmoblat- tina dates as far. back as th 4

PORCELAIN INSULATORS. Porcelain is a substance largely used in electrical insulation, and in a lecture on "Electrical Porcelain " Mr. H. W. Brady set before the Birmingham Electrical Club some interesting facts with regard to this substance. The critical event in the history of the art of pottery, he points out, was the introduction into Europe of the Chinese method of making true porcelain. The deri- vation of the word porcelain from the Latin porcusi" a pig, is an interesting one. It is the Italian " poreellana '' formed from the Latin, and meaning both a shell and porce- lain. It seems to have been applied to a white-surfaced Venus shell, because of its re- semblance to ,a pig's back. From the shell it was . transferred to, the glazed china, which in turn resembled the shell. English porce- lain has greater insulating powers than Ger- man. It has stood high tension tests up to 91)0,000 volts. OXYGEN FOR CUTTING METALS. One of the most remarkable uses of oxygen gas is for the cutting of metals. By means of it such things as steel rails and armour plates can be cut with great ease. The inven- tion is a German one, and it has been in use for several years. The cutting is effected by applying the nozzles of two pipes to the metal. One of these supplies a stream of mixed oxygen and hydrogen, which is ignited, and the other pure oxygen. These strike the same part of the metal, which is rapidly cut through. The cutting is really effected by the intense heat, the temperature rising to I,3oodeg. or I,4oodeg. Fahr. The cut is as clean as an ordinary sheared cut, klnd se- quires., little or no finishing. The chemical and physical properties of the metal are not affected beyond one-hundredth of an inch. , The cost is about half that of cutting in the . old way.

, SNAKE VENOM AS MEDICINE. • ' iOne of the.. Most remarkable medicines over used, perhaps,, is snake venom. And the Scientific American gives an interesting ac- count of how a fpesh supply has been oh-, . tained from a living snake in the New York Zoological Park. The particular snake from li-which it is obtained is known as the lance- head (Laelkesis triganecephalea), and it must ,be taken from the living animal. The snake is held by keepers by two forked stieLs, one at the hack of the jaws and the other at the , tail. A glass beaker covered with gauze is held in front of it. The snake strikes at the gauze and the poison drops into the beaker. After doing this three times 17-1 grains of a yellowish fluid were obtained. One part of venom is mixed with 99 parts of sugar and water. The dose is one-millionth of -a 'slain of venom, and the supply thus obtained is td to be sufficient to supply the world's need for fifty years. It is Used in. the treat- ment of insanity and infectious diseases. The only previous supply was collected by Dr. Herring, in 1828, from. a snake on the Upper Amazon.

APPLETON RIFLE CLUB.

A team of ten members of the above club visited St. Helens last Saturday, for the purpose of competing in a shooting match with the St. Helens Civilian Rifle Club, whose range is situated on the hill at Dentons Green. The weather was very fine, but a brisk wind was blowing, which rendered scoring by the visiting teem a matter of greater difficulty than on their own range, where windage has not to be reckoned for. The conditions of the match were as follows. Each team to consist of ten members, the best eight scores on each side to count miniature rifles. Seven shots at each of three ranges-25, 50, and ICO yards. Decimal scoring highest possible at each range, 70 per man or an aggregate of 9.10 for the three ranges. The match resulted in a victory for the St. Helens Club, who made a total of 13(13 against 1125 by the visitors—the former thus winning by a majority of 238. • MORSE'S MAN Rill PILLS.

THE WAY OF A WOMAN,

(Cop-jr;gl,l.)

BY MABEL COLLINS,

Author of "Lady Ladelle of fere House," "Friend or Traitor? " "Pans T's Lovera," "The Star Sapphire," 1:c.

It was just before dawn—that wonderful hour, when the birds wake and stir, and the trees look ghostly, and the air feel i more ethereal than at any other hour in the day or night. The night had been very hot, but now a faint.breeze was stirring, and it waved the muslin curtain over the open window of May Wolvaston's room. She lay on her bed with her head thrown back and her hands clenched, fast asleep and dreaming. Now and then she uttered some incoherent words, and clenched her hands more firmly, so that the nails cut the skin of their palms. She seemed like a person engaged in an awful struggle, and yet her body lay there, the feet crossed, Motionless save for the moving lips and the quivering hands. She was a beautiful girl, very pale, her hair flaxen, her skin delicately white. Those who loved her often called her "Little White May." Her very lips were white now as if with intense excitement, and it was evidently a release from some dreadful condition when the spell of sleep was broken and the heavy lids were lifted. Eyes, the blue of a Russian violet, looked forth bewildered—very beautiful eyes, filled now with a ' scared look. Tears came into them, large tears, but they only slowly formed and scarcely fell; the girl was too amazed at her own visions to weep over them. She closed her eyes again, and turned her head-from the light. With an effort—not because her memory was failing, but because her memories were so dreadful—she went back over every incident of her dream. She shuddered as she recalled them. She had been standing' in a street between her father and her lover, standing between them and turning fl•oin one to the other, speaking to them, trying to make them hear or feel her, but in vain. Real as they were to her, she was invisible and intangible to them. They were quarrelling bitterly. It was about money I How horrible! How sordid, she thought, as she recalled it. How could such ideas come into her mind, and poison her quiet sleep? Money! Her lover had upon him a roll of bank-notes ; strange, that in her dream it did not seem strange to her that she could see these bank-notes in his breast coat-pocket; the cloth of his coat was transparent to her sight, the money was a large sum; it did not belong to him—he was in charge of it, taking it for his employer to a person to whom it was owed. Her father wanted it —wanted to take it from him! How horrible it was! He' had evidently been drinking, and she knew he had been gambling. What did he want to do.? That money would not pay his debts? No, but it would enable him to get away, to leave the country, to leave his debts, to leave her! Her sweetheart, her dear George, asked 'him if he could find it in his heart to do this—for May would be left alone— in lodgings, almost friendless, and very poor. Her father paid no hoed to him: he wanted that money. She had seen her father like that sometimes—she knew he could be like that. Then came an awful moment. Her father attacked George, who was obliged to defend himself. Her father fought wildly to oderpower him, struggled fiercely to tear open his coat and snatch the notes. But George- was younger,, and he was sober. Suddenly lie escaped from his opponent, and, turning, darted down the street like lightning. May flew to his side, holding his hand. Suddenly there was a great noise; she saw a mail-cart tearing down the street they were crossing. She tried to hurry George on ' more quickly out of its way, but she could not ; she lost hold of his hand, and the next thing she knew was that he had been run over, and lay there in the roadway. The cart stopped, men came running back, there was much confusion and excitement.. and then she saw George lifted upon a shutter and carried away. She followed beside him ; he was in great pain, and unable to speak, which he seemed longing to do. Indeed, she knew what lie wanted to say: he wanted to beg them to take him to the place where the money was to be paid, that he might place it in safety. But he could not speak, and she could not help him; he was carried to the door of n great hospital, and she followed him into it, and up the steps and into the accident ward. She tried to add her strength to his and to help him catch the hand of the surgeon who bent over him and guide to the roll of notes. The doctor nodded, and, leaning over him, said.: "I'll take care of them for you; they are quite safe."

George sank back exhausted with a gasp of relief, and it was at that moment that May woke up, to realise that she was in no hospital ward, but in her own little bed. She was quite tired out. What a strange dream! She lay and thought about it a lung time. Then came the weariness of dragging herself out of bed, of getting up and dressing herself in her shabby dress, of going downstairs to a solitary, dingy breakfast. For father had not been home all night. That had very often happened lately, and she knew it meant misery. She sat and sighed, and wondered what she could do, and thought, now and again, of her strange and dreadful dream. W 'hat did it mean? Did it mean anything? No, she told herself, it meant nothing—only the distress of her mind, the confusion of her thoughts, her great anxiety. She had not yet decided how to spend her day, whether to go out and visit an old aunt—her only friend in all great London—roe whether to settle down to a morning of mending, when a loud knock came upon the door. There was something in that knock which told her that a dreadful event, had occurred, an event which concerned herself. She rose, and stood waiting. Would her father be brought in dead, or in some dreadful condition ? It was a messenger from the police in a different part of London, to tell her that he was dead ; that he had been murdered. In a few moments May, with her hat and cloak on, was going to him—or rather, to his body—it was the only thing she could think of to do. It was a weaiy, dreadfill, sad journey, from a suburb to a busy part of the town, busy, but sordid. ,May hardly spoke ; she was bewildered, she knew not what lay before her, nor what she ought to do.' "Who could have done alien a dreadful thing?" said May. "it muat have been seine kind of accident."

"The doctor thinks it may not have been the !eault of an accident. There are signs of a itruggle, but the injury may have come from a !all."

As he spoke, and hesitated, May began to remember vaguely the horrible dream of the night, which, until now, had entirely passed out of her nemory.

"A struggle!" rhe said, and her lips grew even nore ashen, and she gasped as she repeated the vord.

Mr. Armitage, looking at her, was startled at be sudden change in her face, and wondered what .t meant. Why did this one word fill her with inch horror?

"With whom?" she asked, faintly. "No one knows for certain," said Mr. Armitage, glad that she had given him this opening for what as was waiting to say; "not for certain; but, of :came, the suspicion must fall on the person he was last seen with—especially as there was a piarrel. He had tried to get your father to leave the gaming-table—that was how it was he was with aim so late—and your father resented it. . Thcre was a quarrel, and they were known to be luarrelling as they went out into the street iogether and turned in the direction in which Mr. Wolvaston's body was afterwards found."

"And the other ? " asked May, speaking with the greatest difficulty. "George Dixon has disappeared," said Mr. Armitage. "That is the worst feature in it all. Disappeared, and with a large sum of money upon him, as I know. I have ascertained that it was in his charge yesterday, and that it has not been delivered ; and he has not been seen in his lodgings or anywhere else since the moment when he went into the street with your father." May had fainted. But she was only unconscious for a moment. She fell back in her chair, and her eyes closed. Mr.' Armitage sprang up in alarm, but before he could do anything she had recovered oonsciousness, as if by a great effort of will. "It was not George I" she said, opening the blue eyes full upon Mr. Armitage, and speaking quite olearly, and very positively. "Heaven bless you, child!" he exclaimed. "I'm ;thankful to see your eyes open again--you 'frightened me I" - "It was not George," she repeated. "I like to hear you say that so positively," he said, sitting down again beside her: "it is the way of a woman to believe in her lover against the strongest evidence. But it looks black about George. I must not disguise that from you. It is my duty to tell you so." May rose to her feet; she was not very sieady upon them, but there was a look of fixed resolu- tion in her eyes which showed that she meant to use what strength she had to its utmost. "You must help me' " she said, "for I don't know my way about London. I can find George if you will help me. I want a cab at once." _

Mr. Armitage rose up and gave her his arm, full of genuine gladness and good feeling. "Thank Heaven!" he k aid. "I am more thankful than I can say if George can be found and cleared from this dreadful charge." He led her out of the restaurant, and they walked a little way, till they saw a passing cab. Mr. Armitage stopped it and May got in. "Where shall I tell him to go P" asked Mr. Armitage. "To the largest hospital he can think of—anyone will do—l cannot tell him the name. We may have to go to several before I find the right one." She spoke quietly and with confidence, but all the same Mr. Armitage looked at her in alarm. However, he managed to convey his order to the cabman without letting him think they were going to drive a poor young lady about who had just gone out of her wits from grief and a great shock. He got in and sat beside her, glancing now and again at her half-averted, pale, thoughtful face. What course should he take ? He tried to form a plan, not very successfully. They drove up to St. Bartholomew s Hospital. May looked at the entrance, and shook her head. No, she said, that was not right. She would not get out of the cab. "Please drive to another hospital." This was serious. Mr. Armitage thought his 'really best plan would be to drive straight to a doctor whom he could ask to advise him. But before he could decide on doing this at once, it became necessary to give an order to the cabman; and, indeed, he was himself now so convinced that May was acting under a delusion that he really feared to oppose her till he had quite decided what to do, lest she should become violent! And at the present hour lie feared he should not find his doctor at 'lime. So he decided to drive about a little longer, according to May's wishes, and later on, when he should be sure to find the doctor in, to drive straight to his house. He began to think with some anxiety of his business engage- ments and duties, but his subordinates knew that he had gone out in order to identify Mr. Wolve.ston's body at the mortuary, and would conclude that his long absence was in consequence of matters in connection with this, so he could only go on and hope for the best. Ire felt that it was absolutely necessary to see May into some safe keeping before he left her, even if it took up his whole day. • They went to Charing Cross Hospital, and again she shook her head ; and then started for St. George's. She began to look about her at the streets in a strange, nervous way. "We are not in the right part of London at all," she said presently. "The streets did not look like this." They were turning into Piccadilly when she spoke again. "No," she said, "I am quite sure we are wrong. Do, please, tell him to go to a big . .

• • hosprtal in the midst of wretched small streets witg a great many poor people about." Dlr. Armitage got out of the cab and had a little talk with the driver. He did not wish the man to know the true state of things, nor did he wish him to come to the conclusion that be himself was a lunatic. It took all his tact and ingenuity to manage the conversation, and the man eyed him curiously. However, it ended in the horse being driven down towards Westminster. May leant back silent, watchful; she was evidently becoming very anxious, end Mr. Armitage feared she was getting excited. He writched her furtively the whole time. All her attention was now given to the streets they passed through; hut nothing seemed to really interest or attract her until they had crossed Westminster Bridge and were driving round the corner to the entrance of St. Thomas's Hospital. As they passed the starting-place for the South Lambeth tramcars she leant suddenly forward with a slight exclamation, looking out of the window at the side opposite the hospital; then swiftly turned and looked out at the hospital itself. They were just reaching the entrance. "This is it," she cried out, and began eagerly to try and get the door open, and succeeded in doing so before the horse had actually stopped. Mr. Armitage was in terror. He leant across . and seized her and held her. He might have known from the way in which she submitted to him that she was not out of her senses. "I shall not fall,"

she said, quietly, "I will wait till it stops. Olt, Mr. Armitage, I am so thankful to have found this place I I began to feel so hopeless." "Don't hurry," said Mr, Armitage. "Let me pay the cabmen, and then he need not wait for us." !Truth to tell he was very glad to see he had a chance of getting rid of this man who could not but think he had got a very extraordinary fare—no matter how tactfully he might be talked to. So he held Mary's hand fast in hie while be settled the claim made on him in a much more businesslike manner than the man had at all expected. Ile whipped up his horse and drove away, wondering what that queer couple could be doing at the hospitab.and regretting that he had not made as much out of them as he had expected to do. In fact, he was a wiser man than before, insomuch that he found he was not FO perfect a judge of character as he had thought himself to be. There was something about this queer fare that he did not understand. The cabman -disposed of, Mr. Armitage turned to face the difficulties of entering the hospital, which he thought might be considerable, for he saw that it was not the right time. But, to his great surprise, May, in a perfectly calm and decided manner, now took the lead. She spoke to the porter herself, and explained to him that they had come to see a young man who had, been brought in injured from a street accident during the night. She told him the business was urgent, and in such an impressive manner that he admitted them and sent a message to the house- surgeon. My. Armitage followed her when she led the way, completely astonished at the manner in which she spoke, and also at what she said—for how did she know about this accident—if she did know about it, and if there had been one? For he had heard nothing of it until now, and he felt it as moat probably a mere delusion of the girl's be- wildered brain—but for a moment, now and again, as he heard her sneaking with that confidence

%thich for the time being gives conviction. At one moment it seemed to him that he was follow- ing a mad girl, at another that this girl had never appeared so sane, 83 self-contained and sensible as she did now. It was very curious to pass through these transiticns of feeling, and was cp-ne a new experience in Mr. Armitage's prosaic life. But his sense of surprise, his anxiety, and . - apprehension were all as not 'ling compared to what May was herself suffering, cool and collected though she, seemed. Under that cool exterior she was quivering with fear lest her strange dream should lead her all astray. But she was quite determined to test it to the full. Nothing should daunt her till she stood beside the bed on which she had seen her lover laid in her vision of the night; she vowed this to herself and firmly held her trembling limbs and strained nerves in check. Not till she s'ood beside that bed and saw who actually lay there would she allow them to give way in the very least. Quietly she went on, following each direction given to her, speaking sensibly to everyone ; only when she encountered the house-surgeon suddenly did her nerve almost desert her. He was bending over tannething when he was asked to speak to her ; he lifted himself quickly and turned. That face was perfectly familiar to her. She had seen it bent over George's bed last night! She, could scarcely prevent herself from crying out "You know he is here. You have the money. safe!" but she controlled herself by a tremendous effort. Very quietly she spoke to him, and asked him to take her into the accident ward, saying she wished to speak to a young man who lay there, on most urgent business.. "Probably the young -man who was •run over," said the doctor, looking at her with interest, for the roll of bank-notes made him know that there was something special about this particular patient. "Poor fellow! J. am afraid he will not know you, even if you are a friend of his ; but come with me and we will-see."

They followed him, May's heart beating very fast, and Mr. Armitage full of a growing sense of excitement that was distinctly uncomfortable to a man who was accustomed to a monotonous and orderly life. In a very few minutes they stood beside the narrow bed and looked down upon a prostrate and apparently unconscious form. May fell on her knees beside the bed, and the doctor and nurse both stepped reverently to the other side, thinking she was praying for the life of the man she loved—they had no idea that her limbs had literally given way beneath her from sheer exhaustion and over-excitement, and that kneeling there with her face hidden at the side of his pillow she was as unconscious of the troubles and joys of this world for the moment, as was the man whose wan face lay upon it. Mr. Armitage stood and looked down very steadily at that same wan face. Then the doctor addressed him: "Is this the man you are looking for?" he asked.

"Yes," said Mr. Armitage. "It. is George Dixon, a clerk in the business house where I am chief cashier." He took a card from his pocket- book and handed it to the doctor, who looked at it and nodded. "We have been looking for him with great anxiety, for several reasons ; one, that he was in charge of a large sum of money." "I have a roll of bank-notes locked up in my desk," said the doer, "which he handed to me when he was brougat in. Of course, you know the amount and the numbers of the notes?" "Certainly," said Mr. Armitage.

"Then it 7you will come down-into my room we will go into that matter." De paused and looked at May's kneeling figure. The nurse bent over her and then lifted her up. "She has fainted," she said. • Every effort was made to restore her, but this time it seemed as if she scarcely would return to life. The tremendous and unnatural strain being over, shc lied Sunk completely under the effects of it.. - She was carried into the nurse's room and all possible attention given to her, but each time that consciousness seemed returning it deserted her again, and the heavy lids closed down upon the violet'. eyes. Mr. Armitage stayed beside her, thoroughly. alarmed. The more he thought about the experience he had passed through with her the more extraordinary and utterly unaccountable it appeared to him to be. How could she have known where George Dixon was ? Ile was asking himself this question over and over again when the blue eyes suddenly opened and looked straight at _ _

"How did you know where George Dixon was ?" he said at once, unable to restrain his curiosity any longer. She smiled very faintly, and answered, in a low voice, but a perfectly clear one : "I saw it in a dream—last night. I dreamt that I was with him —in that dreadful street—l stood between him and father in that dreadful quarrel—l was with him when he was run over—l followed him to the hospital. Thank Heaven I remembered it rightly and could find my way to-day!" "This is a strange story," said Mr. Armitage, who had never heard anything like it before. "A very strange one," said the doctor • "but we often hear strange things in a place like this." They said no more to May, for she was too exhausted to talk, and it was some time before she could be taken away from the hospital. Mr. Armitage and the doctor found the money to be all right, and. he went back to his office for a short time ; then he returned and found May sitting up and looking mop like herself. He found her very anxious to see George again. "Surely," she said, "he must be able to see us now, and would know us if we went to him? Do let us ask to see him again." Mr. Armitage went in search of the doctor and got the permission. They were again taken into the ward, and found George lying in a slightly different position, his eyes fixed on the door. He smiled feebly, but very brightly, when he saw May coming towards his bed. He tried to put out his hand to her.

- "1 knew you would come soon," lie said. "I have been looking for you all the time. May, I am anxious about your father. Do you know how he is ? He staggered and fell back when I left him, and I fear he must have hurt himself, but I didn't dare stay another moment. I wanted to get safe away with the money. I saw James Sadler coming down the street after us, and I hoped he would pick him up and look after him. Did he, do you know ? " Mr. Armitage leant across her. "Who is James Sadler? " lie asked. '`May knows," said George, faintly, for he was growing tired. "He was with us at the club and came out; directly after us." The nurse came to warn them that George must not talk any longer. They were advised to leave him for the present. Si) they went away, walking down the ward without a word, both absorbed in thought. But on the stairs Mr. Armitage turned to May quickly and said : "1 must find James Sadler at once. He can clear away all suspicion of foul play. There may perhaps be a chalice now that it need never even be suggested." • "I know where he lives," said May. "Father brought him to the house sometimes. But if he left father lying there it seems strange, doesn't it ? Why didn't lie do something for him ?" They funnel the man without much and he proved to be a mere idle, selfish acquaint- ance of Mr. Wolvastim's, who felt be had done all that could be required of him when he had found a constable and told him a drunken man was lying in a certain street. liat he had witnessed the struggle quite plainly as be approached, awl he was willing to te,tily that Mr. Wolvaston had certainly not been used roughly or been thrown down. lie had swayed back, caught at a lamp- post, steadied himself by it for a moment, and then fallen down helplessly as a drunken man does. He must have been injured in falling against the kerb. kfay slept that night as only the very exhausted do sleep ; a deep, trance-like sleep fell upon her, from which she woke refreshed and strong—and utterly amazed to find herself in a strange room. Mr. Armitage had taken her to his home, and she soon remembered, when she looked round, that she lay in the guest-room of his house. 'They were all very kind to her, and by degrees she recovered her strength, and her natural gay spirits returned to her. She is now a happy wife and mother; and every time the children gather round her they insist oz hearing her tell the story of her wonderful dream. She has had some remarkable dreams since then, but never ont which produced such results, or on which in 'much depended,

Although Sir EDWARD GREY THE RUSSIAN was careful to explain to the VISIT. House of Commons that the Kim's visit to the CZAR OF RUSSLA f.t Revel does not involve any question of a closer alliance between this country and Russia, it is not to be doubted that, in point of fact, the event must tend to encourage sympa- thetic diplomatic relations between St. Petersburg and Downing-street. Russia and England to-day occupy a position towards each other as has not existed since the time when NAPOLEON dreamt his wildest dreams of Empire. For many years it Was a cardinal point in the English political creed that Russia was the enemy at the gates. .Eler gradual march southwards towards the Himalayas and towards the Persian Gulf accounted for the suspicion with which she was regarded in this country. Excited soldiers published alarming accounts of 'the progress of Russian arms on the frontiers of Afghanistan, and the ease with which the conquest of India was to have been accomplished by the Russian troops rivalled the facility with which the Garman Army is to effect the invasion of England according to certain pessimists of the present day. Whatever the dangers may have been, the Acglo-Russian Treaty, concluded some time ago, largely disposed of them. In a sense,that treaty and the improved relations between the two countries may be attri- buted to what at the time threatened to be an episode of evil. The North Sde incident revealed to statesmen in both countries the extreme danger of the situation. In England, Rassia, as we have said, was regarded with suspicion. In Russia Great Britain occupied no better position, and it was evident that, given some cause for national resentment such as that excited by the mistake of Admiral ROZEIE3TVENSKI'S ships, there might be a serious interruption of the peace of the world. It was the realisation of the danger which lay in this situation that made statesmen on either side, and not least the two Monarchs who have just met at Revel, anxious to come to some arrange- ment which might lessen the possible cause of friction between the two countries, and give greater hope of the prolongation of peaceful relations. The main line' of defence adopted by Sir En ‘VARD Gaily in explaining the circumstances of the KING'S visit was that in the changed con- dition the postponement of the visit of courtesy which is due from the KING to the CZAR could not be accomplished without serious diplomatic consequences. Everyone in Eng- land must desire that in the advance of amity between the nations Eagland and Russia I should not stand aside. Between France and Russia there has lona existed a formal alliance, whether to the advantage of either or both the parties it is not our business to inquire. We do not in England desire particularly the cementing of understandings between our neighbours and ourselves by formal treaties binding us to action

or inaction under certain stated circumstances— that has never been one of enduring principles of English foreign policy; but the ambition to live at peace with our neighbours has steadily grown until in a sense it may be called the ruling passion of English diplomacy at the press nt time. That the Czta and the KING have discussed matters of vital importance between their respec- tive countries cannot be doubted. Toe presence of the UNDER-SECRETARY FOR FOREIGN' AFFAIRS, Sir CHARLES HARDINGE, Who Ler some time was Arnbassadir at SG. Petersburg; of Sir JouN- Fisuza, the First SSA Lord of the Almiralty ; and Sr JOHN FRENCH tile Inspector-G eneral of the Forces, in KING EDWARD'S suite, and of M. STOLYPIN, and M. ISVOLSEY'S in the suite of the OAR, gives a clear indication that the coaversa - tion has been of a very serious turn, and that it may have a bearing upon future matters between the two countries which may even go a little beyond the indication given by the FonEroa SECRETARY to the House of Commons.

The English people generally have never, we believs, been actulted by any feeling of towards Russia or her people. Indeed, probably in no country in the world have those who are struggling for political freedom been the recipients of more constant kindness and sympathy than in England. But though we may sympathise with the nation struggling to rid itself of bonds which to our enlightened mind seem unendurable, there could be no excuse for any such interference in the affairs of Russia as has been suggested by certain Members of Parliament representing the working class. The improvement of the relations between this country ,1 and Russia is the more welcome because we believe ' that in the course of time it may give to some extent a much-needed encouragement to those who are trying to guide the forces of democracy along the right lines. The Russian democracy has been taken hold of by the revolutionary ides, but sane politicians recognise that its most powerful advances must be along a medium course.

A letter in another column NON-TaAmNo. from Mr. A. E. Beek shows that he is one of those gentlemen who have signally failed to understand the height, depth and breadth of the Non-Trading movement ,in Warrington. His perspective is singularly at fault when he attributes the " treat- ment " of Mr. H. Roberts to his " coming to a different decision from some others on an arguable principle:' The principle that a Town Councillor's position must be free from all suspicion of personal gain through his membership of that Council is not " arguable," but generally admitted as the first essential of sound and pore municipal government. There were more things than mere diflerencas of principle in the Non Trading agitation. We advise Mr. Beck to read the pro- ceedings and report of the Non-Trading Inquiry held in 1904.

Widnes makes and sells iti THE PRICE OF gas to ordinary consumers GAS, at is. 2d. per 1,000 cubic feet, while Warrington charges 2s. 101 for the same quantity. Slot-meter users get 50 cubic feet for one penny, with free use of all internal pipes and fittings, teeter, and boiling burner, whereas in Warrington, a penny only purchases 6ft. of the precious illuminant. The Widnes gas is the cheapest in the world, and somehow people have got into the way of believing that the production of gas at the same price anywhere else is an irnpossibilty. We fail to see why it should be. Widnes is not obviously a suitable place for the demonstration of miracles and there is no reason to suppose that the production of such cheap gas borders on the miraculous. If other towns could only approach the Widnes figure it would be something in - their favour. Warrington clasrges more than • twice as much and is only six miles .„ distant, while the gas is not equal to the , Widnes quality. If wo cannot turn it out at a less expensive figure, would in not be cheaper v to lay a pipe to Widnes and buy the cheapest gas in the world direct ? It could surely be retailed to the ordinary consumers at a less price than 2s. 103. per 1,000 feet, and still leave a substantial margin 2 for profits. Perhaps the Gas Committee will laugh heartily at the ides. But to the lay mind it looks easier to bring gas from Widnes than water from Vyrnwy.

At the forthcoming Pan- THE WEAKNESS OF Anglican Conference a PREACHING, subject which might be profitably and dispassion ately discussed is whether Anglicans cannot take a leaf out of the procedure of Romanists and introduce an Order of Preachers ? It is time— and we say this emphatically—that every priest permitted to preach publicly should possess special qualifications before undertaking such an onerous duty. Because the clergy are not criticised whilst they are preaching, they must not think that when church is over, there is no discussion. At one time organists used to "play out" a con- gregation. To-day that minor officer must be in possession of musical degrees, or he must show his efficiency by com- petition before being elected to his post. It is all vary well for preachers who cannot preach to seek to draw people together by improving the music.. Fairly good singing, however, cannot • atone for conspicuous weakness in preaching. Many laymen of intelligence, nurtured in the doctrines of the Church of England, refrain from attending worship at the parish church on a Sunday. They make no secret of the fact that they are bored by the sermon. While they admit that the typical Caurch of England clergyman is conscientious, altruistic, and a gentleman, they point to the fact that military officers are trained to give instruction on parade in an audible manner,arid that no actor succ2eds who cannot make himself heard by the gallery. Unfor- tunately, in the Caurch of Eugland, many a youth is not debarred from delivering a sermon as a curate although he is afflicted with a pronounced lisp, and has not even a rudimentary knowledge of elocution. It is not exactly what some curates say, but the pemliar way in which they say it, which makes them later in life as Vicars, when officiating from die pulpit, the chief means of emptying churches.

It.is not often that a Judge

THE CHILD AND takes pert—with approval— THE PUBLIC-HOUSE. in the reversal of his pre- vious decision. That, how- ever, is what has occurred in the case of Mr. Justice Darling, who has just concurred with two of his learned brethren in deciding a point of law in relation to the sale of liquor which is of great importance. The last Liceusina Act provided that children under fourteen years of ago should not be served with intoxicating liquor. The drafting of Acts of Parliament is notoriously loose, and Mr. Justice Darling's reading of the law that this meant to exclude the sale of liquor in a corked and sealed bottle to a child is perhaps excusable. The avisional Court, however, has taken the view that the intention of the Legislature was not so much to prevent a child acting as a messenger as to prevent it from being exposed to temptation in the course of the errand by carrying the liquor in an open vessel. -The desire, if the Divisional Court _ls

to be believed, was that the "sip around the corner " should be abolished. That may represent the extent of the intention of some of the Mem- bers of the last House of Commons, but most assuredly there were others who believed that the very entry of children into the public-house was an evil to be guarded aga'nst, and who accepted the Act under the impression that it put a stop 3ffectually to that contaminating influence. HOW- ever, the Judges have only to interpret the laW) and presumably their reading of it is correct

Temperance people will wish it had been other- wise. OA the other hand, the working man who has come home tired from his work and wants his glass of ale will welcome the permission to send his child for it. Perhaps the knowledge that the bottle hal to be corked and sealed may even be a source of gratification to him, for he will be certain of getting full measure !

There is much more than PENNY POST TO the importance of a clesir- AIIERICA. able business arrangement in the establishment of a penny post with the United States. It is but another sign of the solidarity of the English-. Speaking race. Already there is a penny postage With the British Colonies, though it is only ten Years since the otter orit was made to Canada, Cape Colony, and such Colonies as were willing to take it. But that inauguration set up an anomaly SO ridiculous that it had to he swept away as the most foldish of bars to an English- speaking postal union. A letter could go from England to Canada by way of New York for a penny, where a similar letter, going no further than New York itself, cost 2,';d. Now, as the Postmaster-General has been stile to secure, letters after September next may be sent to the 'United States for a penny an ounce throughout the scale. This, every way, is a benefit. A postal service to the States which has grown by SO per Cent, in ten years, to twenty million letters annually IS surely capable of ernazing developments under the new stimulus. The cost of the reform to the British Exchequer is put at R 130,000. A consider- able proportion of this loss, it is to be hoped, will be recouped out of the greatly-augmented service.

THE POLITICS OF THE WEEK.

[By OUR LONDON CORRESPONDENT.] A. short Whitsnntide recess has hardly affected political activity. In fact, political discussions have been unusually brisk, thanks to extra-Parliamentary incidents of great importance, one being the surprise of the Convention of the United Irish League of Great Britain at Leeds, another the Pudsey election, and a third, the King's visit with the Queen to Royal, to meet the Czar and Czarina of Russia. These events have all been the Cause of political excitement and party embarrassments. Therefore, they have Made the politics of the week decidedly lively. THE PRIME MINIS TER A.ND MR. KEIR HARDIE.

There is something curiously thrilling in those debates which illustrate English freedom of speech at its boldest. Noble impulses, everybody recognised, were behind the speeches of Mr. O'Grady, Mr. Keir ond others, who would, if they could, have held back King Edward from an association which tlty disapproved. It requires courage, after all, to do these things—the courage which has preserved to k Its our freedom. It was the recognition of this fact, no doubt, that brought the Prime Minister to the rescue of Mr. Keir Hirdie in his perilous conflict with the Chair, when he declined "absolutely " to with- draw the word " atrocities" when told that its appli- -cation to the Russian Government and Czar was con- trary to Parliamentary rule. With fine tact and 831:apathy, the Prime Minister explained the Parlia- mentary convention which the blunt sincerity of not aol3' Mr. Hardie, but pome of his Labour Party colleagues, was slow to naderstand and to take refuge , behind. The Labour Party in this respect is like the over-truthful child who hesitates to decline invitations, Which are really not welcome, in the language of polite regret, or to sign a letter with conventional formulas. LABOUR AT ELECTIONS. It does not appear, at the time of writing, that the candidate standing at Pudsey for the seat resigned by lir. Whiteley, late Chief Whip of the Liberal Party, has the approval of the Executive of the Labour Party. Oa the contrary, ft is stated that their National Administrative Council have declared that they cannot recognise him. If, therefore, Ur. J. W. tenson persists in intervening in the contest between Ifr. Ogden, the Liberal, and Mr. Oddy, the Conserva- tive, he does so as an independent candidate, but one Churning local Labour support. AN ARGUMENT FROU BASS & CO.

It has already been shown in this column from time to time how the argument of the Tariff Reformers from hops has gone. The latest bl )w has come from Mr. Gretton, who is a member of the great brewing firm of lass, Ratcliffe, and Gretton. After his evidence it is difficult to see how Mr. Gretton's party could put a 40s. duty on foreign h.ms. Previous witnesses before the flop Industry Committee had admitted that a certain proportion of foreiga hops must be allowed to come into this country. Our brewers can- not do without them. Bat Bass er Co. mae nearly as much of them as of English bops. What is more, Mr. Gretton finds that English hops are deteriorating in quality. The best hops make the best beer ; and the best brewers will have them, whatever they cost. The only result of a tariff to protect English hops will be that your beer will cost you more."

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The public-house boards have been re-furnished with pictorial posters against the Licensing Bill, but we do not imagine that the Bill will suffer much! The British Lion has been captured for "trade " purposes. He looks better unmasked, as he is seen on some of the hoardings. * * _

The Warrington Progressive Labour League is about to enter upon a vigorous political campaign, a series of open-air meetings having been arranged. The speakers will include Mr. Joseph Howes who is always welcomed by Warrington audiences as, an interesting and practical speaker. * We like the dairyman witness at Edmonton who, invited by counsel to say that in the winter time there was a slump in milk, replied : "Slump in milk ! I never put any slump in mine. If I did I should Boon have the inspector after me." Certainly "slutnp" has a mysterious sound, unless you happen to know what it means.

* * * Much interest is being taken by some of the residents of Fearnhead in the doings of several young men in their midst. These gentlemen are indulging in the " pleasure " of camping out in a field, dwelling like the Israelites of old in a couple of tents. Thy are, we understand, endeavouring to live the "simple' life, with a few of the blessings of civilisation thrown in by way of a change. * * * The stream of Chinese from the Rand to China continues to flow, and 2,350 were repatriated in May. The following will, we think, interest our readers : Total number of Chinese imported Largest number on any one date (January 31, 1907) Number on May 31, 1908 Reduction since January 31, 1937 .. 32,189

* Warrington has reason to be proud of the Whit Monday achievements of its two male voice choirs, for each succeeded in bringing home a first prize. The Warrington Male Voice Choir is deserving of special mgratulations on winning the first prize at Shrews- bury for three years in succession. It is pleasing to know that both choirs are in a flAwishing condition, and the work which they are accomplishing for musical culture is beyond all praise. May they continue to prosper and to bring honour to the old tiwn !

* * * The new variation of an old joke was recently told by Mr. Reader Harris, K.C., while speaking at Brad- ford. An eminent divine was appointed to say grace at a big banquet at which Royalty had promised to be present. Owing to the unpunctuality of the Royal guest the company stood waiting. To relieve the tension the gentleman at the head of the toble remarked in a loud tone to the clergyman, who was very deaf, " We have lost a great many eminent lawyers since last we mot, Mr. Dean." Perceiving himself addressed, but not catching the words, the dean mistook the remark as a signal to say grace. So, closing his eyes, be said solemnly, "For this and all His mercies the Lord's Name be praised."

* * * Mr. Harold Smith of Liverpool has accepted an invitation to contest the Huddersfield seat in the Con- servative interest at the next general election. Some of oar readers will recollect Mr. Smith as one of the speakers at the brewers' demonstration in Parr Hall against the Licensing Bill. Others will remember that he is known to fame for two reasons: First, that he is the brother of Mr. F. E. Smith, K.C., M.P., and second that he was the author of the much-quoted statement at Queen's Hall meeting in March, in which he asked every man and woman in the Hall "to pay not a farthing to any tradesman who was in favour of the measure" (Licensing Bill). As far as we know, this comprises Mr. Harold Smith's political experience. It is not much with which to woo a Liberal con- stituency like Huddersfield, and we should not imagine Mr. Sherwell, MP., is much concerned by the advent of his young opponent. *

Lymm, where the May Queen was (rather belatedly) crowned on Thursday, is perhaps beet known gener- ally (says the " Manchester Guardian ") as figuring in the old Cheshire saying about " tearing Lymm from Warburton." In its oldest form the saying is used to illustrate the difficulty of a thing—" It is as hard as to tear Lymm from Warburton." Opinions differ as to the meanings of this. Lytrfm and War- burton are neighbour villages a few miles apart, and they both happen to be singularly unspoiled and quaint villages. One interpretation is that Lymm and Warburton used to form one ecclesiastical parish, and another that the fields of the two parishes are so curiously fixed that it would be very hard to distinguish the separate boundaries. The phrase, "I'll tear you limb from Warburton" is a modern corruption. Another Lymm saying is used to describe anything eaten with great relish—" licked up like Lymm bay," Lymm hay being considered particularly good. * *

The Court of Appeal decided an interesting casual labour point last week under the Workmen's Corn. pensation Act. The wife of a stockbroker, whenever she wanted her windows cleaned, sent a postcard to a man named Hill, who came and cleaned the windows, receiving 6s. Gd. a day and his mid-day meal for so doing. lee day by some mishap he fell from an upper window which he was cleaning, and the accident proved fatal. His brother, Harold Bill, claimed compensation, and the County Court judge held that ho was entitled to succeed. The Court of Appeal reversed this decision. What amazes us is 1 that the County Court judge could have found the householder liable. The Act is perfectly clear that compensation is not payable for casual labour except in the employer's own business. If a builder gives a day's work to a mason and the mason gets hurt the builder is liable ; if the builder gives a dinner-party and the hired waiter cuts his linger the builder is not liable. The employer's business is building, not giving dinner-parties. * * * . _

Consumers of gas by means of the penny-in-the-slot meter not uncommonly use the meter as a savings bank, confident that the extra money found by the collectors will be returned to them.

This habit, according to the " Clas World," appears to have been abnormally developed in Wigan, though consumers have been warned against it. Even children are using the boxes for depositing halfpennies. In the year ended 31st March last, the coins found by the collectors, apart from the legitimate penny, were as follows :

5 sovereigns, 10 half-sovereigns, 4 half-crowns, 455 florins, 502 shillings, 131 sixpences, 250 threepenny pieces, 384 halfpennies, 51 spurious coins. Coins that would not operate the meter wero returned, and those that would operate the meter were exchanged for pennies.

, If was in the booking Ace of a Manchester station on Whit-Monday morning. A long queue of people besieged the window—a queue stretching hack nearly into the street. Most of us were weighted with luggage, and each was anxious lest the train should go before he could work his way to the window. A woman, unencumbered with luggage, was in posses- sion of the window. " Which," she asked, "is the beat line to go on to--?" "Ours," answered the clerk impatiently. "Yes, but is your fare less than the others ?" This was only the beginning of a con- versation which received a fresh impetus from some ingenious new question every time the clerk tried to close it. Soon the packed mass behind got impatient. The George Sheffield Memorial Exhibition. The exhibition of the work of the late Mr. George Sheffield, in oil, pastel, water-colour, sepia and black and white, which had been open for four weeks, was closed on Monday evening. There have been 7,600 visitors to the exhibition, which constitutes a record for the period.

Story of Postal Order.

At the Borough Police Court on Saturday, Robert James Lythgoo, Jolley's Yard, was sent to gaol for one month for stealing a postal order value £3 155.. the property of Samuel Woods, a parcel clerk at the Cheshire Lines Station. It was stated that the prisoner was sent by the prosecutor to the post °face to cash the order. He did not return with the money, and information was given to the police, who arrested prisoner. A search at his house revealed 253. of the stolen money under a mattress.

Leaving Bank Quay Station at 7.15 a.m., the party were provided with refreshments in the saloon. Arriving at Llan- gollen at 9.20 a.m., they were met by conveyances for a circular drive of sixteen miles, visiting the Aqueduct, Vronevsyllte, then on to Chirk, driving lnek along the Holyhead-road to Llangollen, where they adjourned to -the Waverley Temperance Hotel for dinner. After dinner the party proceeded round Plas-N'ewydd, then to Canal Wharf for a sail to Berwyn and Horse Shoe Falls, where the party was photographed by Mr. James Parkinson,of Warrington, arriving back at the Waverley Hotel for knife and fotk tea. In the Tranmere Sailing Club's annual Whit lon- day race, over a twenty mile course, from Rock Ferry to Crosby Lightship and back, the " lone " (Messrs. Thompson and Knowles) was successful in gaining the first prize, a Yachts Dinghy, value £lO, presented by Mr. W. H. Rowlands of Bangor. Darling was first across the line, followed by Eileen and Mikado. The sea was very lumpy on the way out to the ship and a good deal of spray was flying about as the competing boats forced their way out to mark. Six GUINFAS will purchase a fully guaranteed PREMIER. Cxcr.E. Inspection invited.—J. S. GAR- AND, 51,33,& 55 Bridge-street, Warringtoa.—Alvt. Death of Mr. H. C. Kite.

Many of our readers will deeply regret to hear of the death of Mr. Henry Churchill Kite, chief delivery clerk in the goods department of the London and North-Wes'ern Railway Company at Warrington, who passed away on Monday after a brief illness. The deceased was at his work up to a fortnight ago and his death at the early age of thirty-two years CAM, as a shock to his many friends, lie had been in the service of the L. and N.-W. Railway Company from boyhood and was greatly esteemed by al who were assaciated with him in business or in piivate life. He was a staunch temperance worker and held the office of secretary of • the Warrington branch of the Railway Temperance Union. His father, Mr. Jacob Kite, was for many years stationm aster at Thelwall, recently retiring on a pension. The late Mr. H. C. Kite lived at Thelwall, and be will be much missed by the villagers. He was closely ' identified with Thelwall Church. Great sympathy is extended to the widow and three children in their sad bereaveraent. The funeral took place at Thelwall Church yesterday afternoon in the presence of a large gathering. The Rev. T. Pyin Williamson, Vicar of Thelwall, officiated, and the organist played the Dead March in' "Saul." The District Office of the L. and N.W. Railway Company was represented by Mr. I David Banks (chief clerk) and the goods department Iby Mr. R. Townley (agent). Many of the deceased's office colleagues were also present, and six of them, along with six of the villagers, acted as bearers. Many floral tributes were placed on the grave, includ- ing a handsarne one from the L. and N.-W. goods department.

NORFOLK SUITS from 18s. 6d.-Bkmts,Outfitter.-Advt. Daring Thief at Warrington. A youth named Walter Peplow, King-street, War- rington, was charged at the Aorough Court. on Thursday, with stealing a pewter teapot, of the value of ss.' from the shop of Isaac Goldstein, Mersey-street, on May 15th. He was further charged with stealing lead flashing, valued at £2, horn the roof of Messrs. Silcock Bros., King-street. It was stated that the prisoner went into Goldstein's shop on the date in question and offered two carriage lamps for sale. The proprietor was suspicious and refused to buy. After prisoner had left the shop the pewter teapot was missed, and it was ascertained that prisoner had sold it to another dealer. The lead flashing% the subject of the second charge, had been stripped off the roof, and the premises are situated close to the prisoner's house. Prisoner sold the lead to a dealer.—Mr. Arthur Browne, who defended appealed for leniency, and said the prisoner was not responsible for his actions at times.—Prisoner, against whom were previous convictions for larceny, was sent to gaol for four months with hard labour.

PITBL/O BENEB/T.—fiNDAR THII CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN Lexcusnms.—Advt. Death of Mr. J. H. W. Clindinning. - -

We regret to announce the death of Mr. James Henry Walker Clindinning,, which took place at his residence, 1, Roonae-street, on Tue3day. The deceased, who was 72 years of age, was a native of Paisley, and elt/10 to Warrington over forty years ago. He was a skilled accountant and at one time was engaged with Messrs. Bolton, glass manufacturers, and afterwards with Messrs. Robinson and Skinner. For a short time ho returned to Scotland and on again coming to Warrington he took up appoinments first with Mr. J. Lunt, contractor, and subsequently with Messrs. Joseph Monks and Co., shippers. Literature was his great hobby and ho was a frequent contributor to our columns under the nom-de-plume of " lona," many of his letters being on theologi- cal subjects. He had published several volumes of poems. He was a close student of astror orny,and was a strong supporter of the theory of Pamllax.He leaves a widow and one son, who is in Melbourne, Australia. The funeral took place at the Cemetery yesterday afternoon, the Rev. T.W. Barber officiating. Amongst those present were Mr. J. Lunt, Mr, Levi Richard. son, Councillor Flood (Prestwich) and Mr.T. Cowden Reduction ID Sugar. AstmEv's,4,Buttermarket st.—Advt Warrington Choirs' Success. Our two local male voice choirs achieved notable successes on Whit Monday, each of them gaining a first prize. The Warrington Male Voice Choir (conducted by Mr. W. S. Nesbitt) visited Shrewsbury where nine choirs competed, the test piece being " Crossing the Plain ' (Maid tryn Price). The adjudicator (Dr. Watson) made his award as follows, the maximum number of points being 80 :—Warrington Male Voice Choir, 77; Crewe Apollo Male Choir, 76 ; Llandrindod Wells Male Choir, 70; Newtown Male Voice Choir, 68; Birmingham Victoria Male Choir, 67; Birmingham Welsh Gleemeo, 60 ; Aberdare Male Voice Choir No. 1, 60 ; Hadley and District Male Voice Choir, 61 ; Aberdare Male Voice Choir No. 2, 59. Warrington thus won the first prize of £3O and Crewe Apollo the second prize of £lO. A , fact upon which the members of the Warrington choir are to be specially congratulated is that they have won the prize for three years in succession. After the competition the whole of the nine competing choirs, numbering some 400 voices, gave a massed , performance of the test piece under the baton of the composer, which was thoroughly enjoyed by the large and critical audience, which numbered several thousands. At an Eisteddfod, held at Bwlch Gwyn, , near Wrexham, on Monday, the Warrington Apollo Prize Male Choir (conductor, Mr. H. Berrey) were successful in winning the first prize (£2O and a silver crown). The test piece was " The Destruction of Gaza " (De Mile), the judge being Mr. David Evans, of Cardiff who spoke very highly of the way in which the Apollo choir sang the piece.

WHITSUNTIDE PROCESSIONS. . _

I Whit Monday is a great holiday at Runcorn, and the whole of the works are closed for several days, the shopkeepers following suit. Usually there are proces- sions of friendly societies, but on this anniversary these were altogether abandoned. In the afternoon the Sunday School scholars paraded. There was a united ' Church procession, consisting of the Parish Church, St. I Michael's, and Holy Trinity schools, and the Mersey IMission to Seamen. The Nonconformists also combined. As the oldest Sunday School in the town, Brunswick I Wesleyan led the way, and then came St. John's I Presbyterian, St. Luke's Congregational, Bethesda !Congregational, Primitive Methodist, lialton-road Wesleyan, United Alethodist Free Church, Camden Wesleyan, Nelson-street Wesleyan, and Salvation Army. The Catholic School held an independent procession. Afterwards tea was provided at the several schools.

AN OUP-OF-WORK TRAGEDY.

' Mr. Brighouse hold an inquiry at the Widnes Police I Station, at noon, on Tuesday, touching the death of William 'Thomp ' son aged thirty-seven, of 44, Dixon- street, whose death occurred on Sunday morning. From the evidence of his wife, Margaret, it transpired that he had been out of work recently, and on Saturday afternoon deceased said that he was going to get some ' money for a job he had completed. At 10.30 she went to look for him, and found him at his father's house near Appleton Station. About 10.30 he came home and told his wife that he was going to Canada if he could not get work shortly. Witness advised him to go to bed, but he refused, and she weut to bed herself. Being anxious because of his threats to take his life when in drink, she earns downstairs three times, and on the last occasion she found him hanging from a nail behind the door with a handkerchief around his u ‘ck. Assistance was procured, but death took place before the arrival of medical aid. A verdict of " Suicide whilst temporarily insane" was returned.

This was also ordered to lie on the table. WORLD'S CHEAPEST GAS. WIDNES BREAKS ITS OWN RECORD. _ _ _

At the monthly meeting of the Widnes Town Council on Tuesday, it was decided to again reduce the price of g is. This reduction will make the net price to ordinary consumers Is. 2,1. per thousand cubic feet, consumers over five millions Is. for motive power 101. and to the users of slot machines an additional six feet, making fifty cubic feet for Id. These prices constitute a worlds record in the matter of charges for gas, with no decrease is the illuminating quality. Alderman T. S. "[lanais, in moving the minutes of the Gas and Water Committee, said that the profit on the water was only £ IS, this being due to two unusual things • they had to pay £331 more for fuel than last year sn'd they had to put down a costly engine plant at Stock's Well. Mr. Timmis referred to the success of the opposition to the Liverpool Bill. The bargain made twenty-eight years ago with the Liverpool Cor- poration was to remain. Respecting the gas question, he said that twelve months ago he intimated to the Council that they would be able to go through the year without raising the price of gas on account of the increased price of coal. They had done so, and he wt.s able to state that they had made a ma•mincent profit of £4,000, but of this sum .£2,OOS' would be spent on public lighting, and the remainder would be carried forward. The committee estimated that there would be a saving of £3,000 in coal, which should make a profit of £7,000 for the next twelve months, and this -.estimate had enabled them to make the reduction ; arrangements which were above enumerated. These reductions were not made entirely for the benefit of large consumers, but for the ordinary and slot meter consumers also. no biggest consumer of gas for , power was practically the Corporation. The Widnes and , Runcorn Transporter Bridge was the greatest helper in this respect, and in this concern the town had invested £25,000. Mr. Welding: What is the net cost of manufacture. Mr. Timmis : The net cost of manufacture and maintenance is 6.4 d. per thousand, but the cost of manu- facture and distribution, &c., is Is. Old. per thousand. Mr. Davies suggested that the question of reduction be deferred for one month. The profits had hardly been realised five minutcs, and they were not in a posi- tion to vote on the subject until they had gone thoroughly into the figures. Alderman Neil said he did not think it a good arrange- ment to sell gas at lower than cost price. A lengthy debate ensued on the point, but a resolution moved by Mr. Welding, and seconded by Alderman Neil, to refer the minute back for one month, met with the support of only eight members.

PIT WORK DISORGANISED: A " MINIA run RIOT."

The St.. Helens P:iliCe-a;ii-rt—s;:a.;tfirrOnged on Tues- day with miners, when nine colliers and drawers, living in the districts of Buttonwood and Bold, were summoned 1 by the Collins Green Colliery Company for breaches of the Coal Mines Regulation Act, and impeding the working of the Bold Colliery on Tuesday, April 24th, Mr. Darlington (Paine and Darlington) prosecuted, and Mr. Linden Riley (instructed by Messrs. Riley, Rally, and Pic:.on) defended on behalf of the Miners' Federation of Groat Britain, whose district representa- tive, Councillor Parkinson, J.P., of Wigan, also attended.

The defendants were Joseph Reyes, Timothy Traverse, John Birchall, lhomas Woods, Reuben Southward, IVa!ter Baker, I iui.s Rigby, James Tinsley and James Pennington, and they were ea n summoned on four informations.

Tile offences score—first, travelling the motion brow whilst the htulage ropo 10 inotiou ; second, passed sloog a put of the ruins that was nit necessary for his ' work; third, interfered with .I.isepti Ilimi,ser,ttie looker- on, in tho discharge of his duties ; fourth, impeding the I work of the mine. _

Mr.Darlingtou stated the facts,and the charges against Southward were first dealt with. He said that early on this day a strand in the haulage rope which kept the Florida Mine going broke. The mishap was brought to the notice of Dixea, the undarlooker, who wont to see that the rope was repaired. It was repaired, but during the time the rope was stoppod the men working in the mine became short of boxes, and a lot of coal at:cumu- lated in their working places. At ten o'clock in the morning Mr. Webb, the uader-intnager, went t 3 the men who had left their work and found them coming up the brow in which the rope was then in motion. He remonstrated with then, and asked them to go down to work again, and promised that he would sea that they had as many boxes as possible and every opportunity to make up the time they had lost. The men refused, and thirty or forty of them swarmed up the road. The running of a mina was absolutely dependent upon discipline, and on this occasion the men by their com- bined ection created a miniature riot and completely stopped the working of the pit. The Clerk asked if the four charges were laid as alternate or soparate offences? Mr. Darlington said they were separate offences. Mr. Riley suggested that the four charges really all related to the one offence, Mr. Darlington said the offences were separate. First of all the men left their work and congregated in the motion brow, usei bad language to the officials, and, in spite of the rules aad orders of the olficials, left their work and travelled up the motion brow when the rope was going. That was the first offence. They then went along to the pit bottom and threw the place into dis- order. They threw some empty boxes about the place, and prevented Bamber, who superintended the winding, from going on with his work. They then followed the under-manager round to the east side of the shaft and threw some waggons of coal off the rails. The result of all this was that the pit was stopped, aud 'during forty minutes only one winding took place, and that interference with the working of the pit con- stituted the fourth offense. Altogether thirty-eight men were congregated round the bottom of the shaft for over two hours, and the trouble practically became a riot. After the under-manager had done all he could to restore order, be was compelled to sanction the men going up the pit. Accordingly their names were taken, and they were allowed to go up. lie asked the bench to make an example of these men, as a disturbance like this was a very serious matter both for masters and men.

---.1-o-hn William Dixon, the under looker gave evidence. For the defence Mr. Riley alleged that the colliers were allowed, nearly every day, to travel along the motion road whiie the haulage was in motion. Ms was clearly against the rules, but it was not for the colliery oilicials to allow it to take place on 361 days of the year, and for their own purpose to make it an I offence on the 365th day. Ile would call thirty-eight witnesses to prove that the rope was stopped at the time the men actually went along the road. All the summonses were dismissed for travelling along the rood whilst the rope was in. The charge of interfering with the looker-ca was then gone into. Mr. Riley urged that the men's liberty was being interfered with. They were waiting for boxes for several hours, and as they could not get them they were obliged to leave work, lie called witnesses who swore that the boxes at the pit botto:u wo:e not upset by these MO, but by the olicials, who got into a temper. The bench decided to convict in one case and fined Southward 21 and ISs. costs, and dismissed the other cases on payment of costs. Mr. Darlington withdrew the other charges.

WOFIES. A serious accident occurred on Tip3day, to S 15 year- old apprentice of Messrs. T. arid T. Vicars, named Peter Patter, of Regent-street, Earlestown. It is presumed that whilst he was in the act of placing a strap On a pulley his hand slipped and the belt pulled his arm completely off frorn the elbow, then falling back on to some castings he fell on his right leg, breaking it.
