Mr. BILLING E in moving the adoption of the minutes ' said the Committee felt that if the Local Government Board knew that the House Committee which dealt with the tenders consisted of twenty-two of the twenty- eight guardians, it might influence their decision. The Local Government Board knew the arrangements in force hut they did not quite understand them. if the local Government Board insisted that the seal of the accepted tender should he broken in the 'Board room when the tenders came before the Board, they would have to take filet course. The CLERK: What you have explained is precisely what has been stated to them. Continuing he said every one knew what Was taking place in connection with certain Local Government Ponrd inquiries and that the great evil had been that a few members of a large Boerd had had entire control of the acceptance of contracts with such results as were now being revealed. lie knew that the Local Government Board did not for one moment suspect the Warrington Union hut they had to deal . with the principle itself end if it were lawful atad proper to refer tenders to a committee of twenty-two, then it was lawful and proper to refer them to a committee say of half-a-dozen. The principle of the law was that the Board of Guardians should receive at their Board meetings tenders for contracts which ehonld be eneened in the presence of the whole Board. That was the principle which the Local Government Board wanted to enforce, because if they gave way to the Warrington Union because of certain customs which in spirit were not against the law, how could they refuse tbe same thing to other Unions? lie recommended the Board to instruct him to write stating that they would take note of the Local Government Board's recom- mendations. Mr. MATTIF,R seconded the adoption of the minutes. Mr. A. lieesetcs said the recommendation of the House Committee was very natural and there was nothing out of the ordinary in asking the Local Govern- ment Board to consider what the House Committee proposed. He should support the confirmation of the minutes exactly as they stood. When the new revile- Hots for the acceptance of tenders were formu- lated, the idea was to keep the prices of unsuccessful tenderers secret. There Wes a feeling amongst the Guardians at that time that a great many traders did not tender because their prices were made known all over the towmand it was to obviate that sort of thing that the regulations were brought into force. He believed they had had a very beneficial effect in getting good tenders from people who would not compete under the old conditions. The Local Government Board should be approached on the lines suggested by the House Committee. They could only refuse. 'l'hn Guardians had given way on the point of burning the unsuccessful tenders which would in future be kept under seal and produced to the auditor. The CLERK said he did not like to he the medium of putting before the Local Government Board a segges- tion that they should act illegally or allow the Guardians to act illegally, lie would, however, do so if it were the Board's instructions. Mr. FURNESS said he did not see much difficulty in following out the suggestions of the Clerk and comply- ing with the Local Government Board's requirements by having the tenders opened before the whole Board. It would then be within their province to relegate the tenders to the House Committee for selection. It was ultimately decided that the portion of the minutes referring to the tenders be referred back to

the House Comrattee. The remainder of the minutes were approved. CONFERENCE REPRESENTNTIVES. HIS NERVOUS COLLAPSE Reduced Him to a Wreck.

A Despairing, Nerve-Stricken Man, after Months of Misery, finds a Complete Cure in Dr. Williams' Pink Pills.

The fact that he was a victim of Nervous Exhaus- tion was not realized by Mr. HARIIY TIMEX , of 19, Alina-stieet, Aston, Birmingham, during the several months he was t.rmented by severe headaches, great depression of spirits and acnte debility. Then, when a sudd,n nervous collapse developed, his condition became so serious that there seemed to he no hope of a cure, or even relief. Mr. Truetnan is well to-day and undisturbed by nervous disorders, having been completely cured by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, his only regret being that he did not turn to this splendid nerve remedy at an eat liqr stage of his troubles _ - -

" I liad been subject to a languid worn-out feeling for manilla," said Mr. Truernan, " but did not connect this oith he headaches, depression of spirits, and debility that hung over •me. A doctor supplied me with tonics. but gradually I became more and more miserable an I weak.

'clpless." - " After some months I suffered a sudden nervous collapse that reduced me to a wretched plight. Then Nervous Despepsia seized me, every morsel of food causing agony. Dry weight steadily fell from 11 tst. to 3 st ,and I had only strength enough to walk at a crawl. Though the doctor sail, Don't despair,' I felt helpless, and my shattered nervous system caused me such acute misery that I dreaded to be left alone ; yet I disliked company. Gong upstairs made me breathless, while my hands trembled and I had featful attacks of dizziness. " One evening I read in a booklet the messages of men who told how new health had been found in Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. I ceased taking ordinary medicine and began with these pills. After taking two boxes I was able to sleep better at night and free from nervous disturbances. Every day saw some improvement. Gradually the pains of dyspepsia declined, my food nourished me, and I knew I was being cured. After a few boxes I recovered my full strength, a good colour and my lost weight. Ever since then I have felt well."

Saturday was an ideal day for cricket and local devotees of the summer pastime were busily engaged. The Warrington eleven fulfilled a League fixture with Newton at Arpley, but the numerous supporters of the Warringtonians who assembled wera doomed to dis- appointment, the visitors triumphing by 21. Warrington played the strongest side which has done duty this season. Going to the wicket first, Newton amassed 135, the feature of the innings being the fine display of IL Lyons who carried out his bat after making 65.

W'arrington made a p3or start, two wickets being down for seven runs. The prospects improved, however, when .7. Callon and T.Wright became associated. Wright was in brilliant f orm and ran up 55 ‘,efore he was caught by Harrison,while Callon made 15. The Rov.Horace Gray again appeared in the Warrington ranks but the great expectations created by his grand inning of the previous week were not realized.as he was caught off the first ball ty W.Wright. W. H. Pocimore, who was suffering from an injured hand, contended himself with keeping his wicket intact and his long stay only produced 4 runs.

Warrington 2nd continue their victorious career, Newton 2nd falling easy victims on Saturday. Warrington made 155, S, Baxter (53 not out) and P. B. Hawtin (49) batting splendidly. Newton could not make much headway against the Warrington bowlers and their .innings closed for 59. Bennett captured. 4 wickets for 21 runs, Thewlis 3 for 29 and Warren 3 for 4.

In the Warrington and District League St. James's accounted for the" Guardian "eleven by a majority of 25, W. Minards (14) and J. Cartwright (13) being the top scorers. Wilcierspool Brewery accounted for Rylands' Bros. with 9 runs to spare while Bewsey Wesleyans trounced Walker's Brewery by 61.

There were two matches in connection with the Warrington and District Junior Leagve. Warrington Excelsiors were triumphant over Orford, the most successful batsmen being H. Wilcox (18) and J. Ditch- field (16). Marsh-house-lane inflicted a defeat on Lovely-lane by a majority of 34, a feature of the game being the great array of "ducks." For he winners Mann took five wickets for one run and Llramhall fiVe for two.

Howard took five wickets for 36 runs, IL Craddock three for ten, and G. McGowan two for six.

Monday's proceedings were opened by same very fair bowling. Tommy Glover played his usual clever game and his opponent never had a look in, only scoring six shots. Plinston, who got as far as five and then stuck there. The game between J. C. Howe, one of the past winners, and A. Gleave, who is greatly fancied this year. was most exciting, the latter having a good lead at 18 -12, but Rowe, playing a fine uphill game, actually got in front at 20-19. but lost by the odd chalk. Another fancied man in T. Ellison, easily got the better of the proprietor, J. Allison, winning 21-7. One of the best wins of the second round was that of young W. Fielding over Harry Bulks, who is undoubtedly -11, bit smart at the game, and Fielding should feel well pleased with his performance. Joseph Harrison gained a victory over R. Fairbrother by 21-12.

On Wednesday the second, third and fourth rounds were completed. Warrington representatives were well to the fore and achieved some creditable victories. T. Cross, Heatley) by 21-7, whilst A Gleave (Bull's Head) had to bow the knee to G. Ball, jun., who won by 21-13.

Attention has rightly been drawn to the advisability of numbering competitors in competitions connected with the Associations. If the system of pinning num- bers to each competitor, as has been the custom at sports meetings, were adopted by the L. and C., it would be of great assistance to the spectators who attend the Association's gatherings slid are not familiar with the players.

In connection with the local works 'competition, the first round for the Finlayson Cup was continued on Saturday, when Crosfleld's No. 2 beat Naylor's (165-78) and Monk's Hall defeated Ryland's No. 2 (147-133).1 Crosfleld's got the upper band of Naylor's from the beginning and were only unsuccessful in two games. One of the closest contests was that in which J. Dobson defeated W. Taylor by 15-11. Dabson started exceed- ingly well, having twelve points to his credit at the eighth end to Taylor's one. Taylor then made headway, getting ones and twos whilst Dobson remained at twelve. At the fifteenth end the game stood in Dobson's favour by one point (12-11), and as two more ends were likely to close the gone there was intense excitement,but the soap representative got three points in that time and so ran out winner by 15-11. T. Jackson defeated E. Lunt in grand style by 15-0. Another crushing defeat awaited J. Barnard (Naylor's). His opponent (D. Stokes) gained all the points except one, and so was victorious by 154. Two other of Naylor's men (J. Block and C. Ward) were also severely trounced, getting only two points each. The captain of the same team (J. Howarth) was also defeated by 15-5.

Rylands No. 2 suffered a reverse at the hands of Monks Dail No. 1, who were victorious by 117-133. 11. Newton (Rylands) le all the way in his encounter with B.Dutton.The former did not let his adversary even catch up to him and so won by 15-11. F. Scarisbrick played well for his eight. His opponent (A. Worrell) got the first point but Scarisbrick registered the next three. They after wards drew level (5-5) hut Worrell got ahead and finally won by 15-8. E. Blackledge (Rylands) was severely defeated by W. Thomason. Blackledge got the first point—but it was his last. The game wee finished at the eleventh end. &Rowlands (Monks Hall) looked as if he was going to defeat H. France by a large majority. Rowlands was successful with lila wood and they ran on alternately for a couple of encl. when France got ahead of R iwlands and after an excit- ing finish ran out victorious by 15-12.

Cycling.

We know for a fact that many riders have tired of the old-fashioned oil cycle-lamps, with their tendency to iog out and their liability to mess one's fingers and smell more or less vilely. There is a future for the lamp which is made to eliminate these faults. We have recently been using one of the well-known "Neverout" cycle lamps marketed by the British American Co., of Coventry. This lamp burns paraffin: or to be more correct, paraffin vapour. The reservoir is packed with cotton-wool and you pour as much paraffin as the wool will absorb. There is consequently no grease once the lamp is filled, and the light given is splendid. The lamp never smokes and cannot explode, while in appearance 'whether illuminated or " out" it is most handsome. Each lamp is supplied with detachable brackets so that it can be attached to either the head or fork of the machine, according to the rider's desire. When the ,1 shorter days come many riders will find they require a new lamp. Try the " Neverout."

G. A. 01ley who is making an attempt on the Land's End to John o'Groats' record, arrived at Warrington at 12.22 p.m. on Thursday and left at 12.45, going strong. This time was two hours better than the previous record, 011ey having left Land's End at 10.40 on Wednesday morning.

-- A glorious holiday, with the time all too short, was the unanimous verdict of the members of the Warring- ton Cycling Club on their return !min the Whitsuntide tour.

Leaving Warrington on Saturday noon they travelled rola Chester and on to Ellesmere, making this lovely spot their centre, from which the various places of interest in the vicinity were explored. The weather, not too hot, Was ideal from a cycling point of yiew,anil altogether the Whit tour of 1908 will long be remem- bered by all those who were fortunate enough to par- ticipate in it.

Cuerdley was the run down for decision on Tuesday evening, the members engaging the Wjdnes Cycling Club at bowls on their native heath. Owing to various causes only a very moderate number put in an appear- ance, with the result that the Warrington boys did not come out on top. Eloviever, they hope to make amends in the return match which will be decided in the course of a week or two.

Nearly 40 members were to be seen out at Sankey on Wednesday evening, where the handicap was in full swing, and some very good games were witnessed.

Through an alteration in the Club programme Hunts Cross will be again visited to-day, and the run to Bollington will most probably be carried out later on in the season.

RUGBY nOTBALL.

Northern Union Meeting

Before the annual general meeting of the Northern Rugby Union in Huddersfield on Thursday evening a meeting of the General Committee was held, with Mr. Ashton, of Warrington, in the chair. Permission was given to the Hull Kingston Rovers and the Hull Football Clubs to make a charge at a practice match in August, the proceeds to be devoted to Hull medical charities. The Treherbert F.C. was admitted into membership of the Union. The follow- ing were the dates allotted for cup-ties :—Qualifying stage: First round, Decembec, 12 ; second round, December 26 ; third round, January 16 ; fourth round February 13. Competition proper, February 27; second' round, March 13 ' • third round, March 27 ; semi-final, April 10 ; final, April 24. Messrs. Smith, Platt, Ashton, Cooke, Lyon, and the president were deputed a sub- committee to deal with all details connected with the visit of an Australian team during the coming season Later the annual general meeting of the Union was held. Mr. Ashton again presided. A statement of accounts for the past season showed a balance in hand of £1,056. During the season grants amounting to £317 were made in respect to junior and school foot- ball, and a sum of £79 was voted for charitable pur- poses. The Northern Union game, says the report, has given great satisfaction in Wales, and three additional clubs, Gorseinion, Treherbert, and Mid- Rhondda, have notified officially their inten- tion to join the Union. The visit of the Now Zealand team was an undoubted success the share paid over to the visitors being £8,838. " Their visit," the report adds, "has done an immense amount of good to the game, not only in England but at the further ends of the earth. Strong Leagues have been formed to play our game in Australia, a representative team from which Continent will be with us next season, when the Committee trust that the same enthusiam and good feeling will be shown towards them as was to their predecessors from New Zealand." The report and balance-sheet were adopted unani- mously, and the meeting proceeded to the consideration of proposals by various clubs to alter by-laws, rules, and laws of the game. Nothing of importance in this way was done. Proposals by Salford, Swinton' Ebbw Vale, Wakefield Trinity, and Halifax with regard to the constitution. of the Union Committee were lost, and the Committee remains constituted as before. A proposed additional law that senior clubs should have prior claim upon the services of players in their local mtertuediate 12agues vrus. not carried, the feeling being

that it was unfair to bind any young 'player and iireirnit him from doing the best be could for himself in the choice of a club and in the matter of wages. By a curious oversight the scoring of tries is not recognised in Law I. of the game setting out the object of play. " The object of the game," it says, "shall be to kick the ball over the crossbar and between the posts." Try scoring is not mentioned. The omission (which, if memory serves, exists also in the Rugby Union rules) was remedied by the insertion of the words" to cross an opponent's goal line to score tries" before the words "kick the ball," &c.

-- An interesting discussion took place upon a pro- posal by the Union Committee that the use of studs affixed to player's boots be prohibited. Instances were given of severe injuries having been caused to players by boot studs, and it was said that players were known to pare their studs to a point (with a view to making them more effectual in preventing slipping) till they became a source of greatest danger. A number of opposite views were expressed in the discussion as to whether bars were as effectual as studs. .1. Goldthorpe, a well-known foottialler in his day, said he found bars absolutely use- less. He thought they might as well give up the game altogether as abolish the use of studs. Mr. M`Cntcheon' of Oldham, an international three-quarter little more than a decade ago, said he never used any- thing hut bars, about which there was nothing danger- ous. He considered them less dangerous than studs. Other players of note agreed with him. In the end, however, the proposal to abolish stubs was lost, It was suggested that the Committee might do some- thing to fit a standard size for the ends of stues, thereby preventinir, players from whittling them down to a dangerous point.

PEEBLES HOTEL HYDROPATEM.

For the combined advantages of thorough enjoyment outdoors and indoors, and permanent restoration to health, few holidays can compare with one spent M the Peebles Hotel Hydropathic. Ail its appointments are on the most sumptuous scale, and whilst being on the lines of a modern, high-class hotel, has at the same time that home comfort which is so delightful. The cuisine is of the highest order, and meals are served in the spacious dining broom at separate tables and with every luxury. The surroundings and scenery of Peebles are amongst the most beautiful in Scotland, the view from the terrace of heather-clad hills is one of exquisite beauty. The neighbourhood is full of historic interest, and there are delightful walks' motor rides, coach drives ift all directions. The roads are good for cycling. Good fishing may be obtained in the adjacent waters of the silvery Tweed. Every variety of amusement is to hand, and out of doors lovers of croquet, tennis, bowls, etc., can find ample opportunities in the spacious grounds. Close by a new 18-hole golf course has been opened. In the extensive cellarage every accommodation is given for motors. Motor cars are on hire, and daily excursions amongst delightful scenery are made by motor char-a-bancs. These are a few of the pleasures that await healthy holiday-makers. A complete system of baths of all kinds, together with the latest forms of electrical treatment are installed, and large numbers from all parts of the country have materially benefitted from these advantages. The social life of the hydro is a great feature from its very incep- tion. The recreation room is capable of accommodating about 300 for dancing, and all kinds of high class enter- tainments take place here after dinner. The Lounge which is so important a feature in modern hotel life, is fitted up with every luxury, and is highly appreciated by both ladies and gentlemen ; it forms the centre for indoor social life for the guests of the hotel. There is a large Billiard Room with three modern tables, and all the appointments tend to every comfort and luxury. An illustrated brochure is issued contain- ing exterior and interior views, • and can be had on application to Mr. Thiem, the managing director, by. post by any of our readers.

" PRINCESS " A,T THE WHITE CITY.

A beautiful grey horse, no different in appearance from the many hundreds which are seen daily, but one possessing a mind almost human, has fairly captivated thousands of lovers of dumb animals, as well as set thousands of Whitsuntide visitors guessing as to whether or not a horse could possibly he gifted with the power of thought. The horse in question is " Princess Trixie," the peer of any trained horse in the world, whose marvellous performances startled all London, and brought about a commend to appear before the Royal Family at Windsor. Thousands of letters have been received by Mr. Harrison Barnes, trainer and owner of the gifted 1" Princess Trixie " from sceptical persons seeking information as to just how he taught his animal charge to display such remarkable intelligence. Men, women, and children, have flocked to the quarters of the " Princess "daily and applauded vociferously when the " Princess " without the slightest cue from her master, told the time of day by looking at the watch of a person in the audience, picked out van-coloured cloths to describe the costume of ladies present, counted the audience, spelled the names of many present, and, then wound up her performance by making change on a cash register for sums of Toney. Another free attraction which will delight the patrons of the White City is listed to begin June 22nd, when the Three Rohrs,the most daring cyclists in all England will give a sensational bicycle act, the equal of which has not been seen for years.

MY DMV

I am pleased to say that the ... are coming in very well indeed. I hope to recciva a few more during the week, and shall then have pleasure in publishing the best re- ceived. I cannot promise to send a prize to all senders of stories, poetry, etc., but only when special competitions are announced; but I am, always pleased to send a little pocket mirror to any member, if requested, whose contribution is published. Now, I am not going to take up much.space this week, as such a lot of stories, etc., have conic to hand, and I want to publish c.s many as possible. With 'love, UNCLE NED.

I really don't think there is any place so good for a real game of romps as a hayloft, because you can't hurt or spoil anything. and you can't—at least, not very easily—hurt your- selves.

When I was a little girl we had a very -big hayloft, and many were the happy days we spent there. Our favourite game was explorzers. We used to set off on a journey through the hay, from one end of the loft to the other. fay brother Ted went first, because his strong arms made a path through the forest, as we called the hay, then came Doris the baby, and then I came last of all. "To-day," Ted would say grandly, "we will go to the North Pole," and off we, used to start quite happily. Now and again, for a great treat, mother let us have our tea in the hayloft. Oh, what fun it was! And how we laughed when dust got into the tea and hay into the sandwiches.

THE LIFEBOAT.

It was in the montn of April, six years ago. Theze was a great storm off the Yorkshire eat. The fishermen who belonged to a little iilay on the coast were out at sea. They wele in great distress, and made signals to the people, on shore. The waves were mounting hi,J.ll over their boats, and they would • soon be z ed. The lifeboat must be put out to save them. . But these men in danger were them- selves the crew of the lifeboat. -

The men and women of the village were gathered on the shore. There was a call for men who would go out in the lifeboat. Enough men at once stepped forward to risk their own lives for the sake of their friends. The women, too, did their share. They helped to push the boat out. This was a work of Jam-,er. The women had to wade into the sea, and were beaten about by the great waves. Then they waited until their brothers and 11,1C.Iand3 were brought safely to the beach. What gladness there was then! The above was told to one of our representatives by Mrs. L. Bolton, 7, Oldham-street, Latchford, Warrington, over eighteen months after we first heard of the benefit she had derived from Doan's pills. In describing her case, just after she sas cured, Mrs. Bolton said :—" When I began with Doan's pills I was almost doubled up with the pains in my back. If I stooped at all, my back was so weak that I could not get up for some minutes, and then I had to stand still for a while. At other times the pains caught me like stabs from a knife, and I felt liko falling. "But since taking Doan's pills I have lost all the pains, and my back is much stronger. I am better in every way, cud Doan's pills are the only medicine I have to thank, as before I used them no n..edicine did me any good. (Signed) L. Bolton." NEW BRIGHTON TOWER. The Whitsuntide attendance at New Bfighton Tower set up a new lot of records all along the line for amusements at that popular resort. The total paid attendance was nearly 51,000 and everyone of the many shows, rides, devices etc., in the grounds was taxed to its utmost capacity by crowds of pleasure seekers. The figure 8, took in more money than any Figure 8 in America or Europe ever did before. The Cummins Brown Wild West gave six performances in order to satisfy the constant crowds crying for the Show. This entertainment has certainly hit the public fancy and every day sets constantly increasing and delighted audiences at. the at the perforrnan3es. Nlanager J. Calvin Brown made no error in bringing over to England this big company of Indians, Cowboys, Bucking Horses etc. To-day the 10th Annnal Brass Band Contest for the Tower Cup Value £lOO and some cash prizes will take place at the Tower on the open plaza. Twenty of the best bands of the district are entered and an equal number of excursions will he run to the Tower, for the interest in these band competi- tions is very great throughout all the North of England.

MR. DODDS AND MR. ROBERTS.

Mr. A. E. Beck Discusses the Non-Trading Issue. ‘• A Question of Principle"

The Tariff Reform League and 1 Unemployment. Sir,—My criticism of the Tariff Reform meeting has , produced further proof of my contention. W. Chriraes; one of Alderman Bennett's foolish virgins, states in ' . your last week's issue that my " figures may be approxi- Sir,—l have read with very great interest the page inately correct " etc. ; also "if we assume for the sake .of your issue of Saturday last, devoted to the COll. of argument, that the state of affairs in America is as troversy between Mr. Dodds and Mr. Roberts. bad as be says, there is no evidence that it is due When two men of undoubted personal and public primarily or.,;generally to the fiscal policy which America probity like Mr. Dodds and Mr. Roberts disagree, , s Hro adPptteed ction will not " primarily " or " *euerally " usually the disagreement can be "fined" down to three affect the unemployed problem, theil what 'earthly use issues. Either there is a different decision arrived at is it to the worker ? It is not oil that is required. about some very arguable principl re, o ' a difference ay ; ; ; .3 .T 1 sore DOA of a new lamp. arising cut of terms used' or 'the truth lies mid-way I Reverting to the open-air meeting the following between.' picture fills my mind. Alderman Bennett speaking about Now in this ca-se the desirability of the end to be ! the glorious Empire. A child scrambling on the achieved-that of Mr. Roberts taking up his public warggon, with dirty face, unkempt hair, ragged clothes, Gloriouso is mspviarneteicridifedre.! il:oVhaptromtnooctkertyh ,4; work in Warrington again-seems to be admitted on , S.lemeeeptr. otectin all hands, even by Mr. Dodds himself, with, however, children's feet, to feed their stomachs. Protection in his case a reservation that Mr. Roberts should make against the rapacity of the rich lor-ls, employers, and some alteration in his private ownership of interest in ' moneylenders, who impudently pose as champions of a limited company to satisfy Mr. Dodds' conception of patriotism and the expansion of the Empire. certain principl ANTI-PROTECTIONIST. regard The attitude of Mr. Dodds with I regard to this principle is known in.Warrington by the phrase "Non-Trading." Now, I would like, with I all respect, to point out to Mr. Dodds that men who I are totally disinterested in the practice, are opposed to him in opinion on the principle. A town for its business management wants its best business men. A company for its directors wants also the best business men. A company would be restricted in its selection, if it decided not to have public men as directors. A . town would be still more restricted in its selection if it said it would not have as councillors any men who had interests in, or were on the Board of limited companies. The suggestion exists that no comrany having any member of the Town's 'Council as director or large shareholder should tender for any portion of the town's work. This suggestion does not solve the matter. For, if such companies are excluded from: tendering for the town's needs, it is readily conceiv- able that in some cases the best sources of supply ; might be cut off. The competition would in all eases inevitably be decreased and the town might pay more, or have lower quality material supplied,: possibly both. How then as the town's interest to be protected not only from anything improper, hut from the possibility of anything improper? The question naturally arises how is the matter treated , elsewhere? For Warrington is the only town I know' of which has a " Non-trading "party. In other towns they elect their best men, and if, as frequently happens, a member is interested in a Corn- pans, having, or seeking to have business transactions with the town, that member retires whilst such matters are being discussed. The same conduct is pursued by l a member on a bench of magistrates, and even on a bench of judges in the Court of Appeal and in the House of Lords. A judge does not say "I am inter- ested in many Companies whose cases may come up for trial, therefore I cannot be a judge." He says " I will state that I am interested, and retire from consideration of the matter when such a case comes before the Court," and our standard of legal justice is the highest the world knows. I state all this to show Mr. Dodds that on this principle other men have come to a different decision from himself and that they are as honestly inspired as he may be. The town's safeguard is not in excluding the best men from service, or the best firms from supplying—it is in electing men of sufficient weight and quality to keep the town's busitiess above reproach. In this Warrington has not done all it could. The personnel of the Council has been a matter of comment in other towns. It is improving -but it is not likely to be improved if men like Mr. R7oberts are to be treated as be has been, for coming to a different decision from some others on an arguable principle. For, bearing in mind that he undoubtedly believed that he was right in principle, nothing which was done was either dishonourable or improper. If Mr. Roberts thought that he could give his best service to the town it was not inconsistent with the thought that the firm he was interested in could give the best value to its customers. It was fa.' the electorate to decide as to whether his services were the best they could get, and they did affirmatively. It was for the Council (apart from Mr. Roberts) to decide as to whether the value from the firm or any firm was the best they could get, and they did -in some cases in the firm's favour. There is not the slightest suggestion that the price was higher, the quality lower or the value less than other firms offered. Presumably Che act of decision was the act of the whole Council or the Committee and one could not impute anything to Mr. Roberts without imptigning every other member. But Mr. Roberts appears to have taken a broad- minded view of the position, and in effect has said : " I still adhere to my reading cf the principle and maintain that I have always acted so as to deserve and retain the respect and confidence of my fellow- townsmen—but I admit that some others read the principle differently and as I wish to secure the good will of all I will arrange my private matters to satisfy even those who differ from me, so that I shall be above even attack." For Mr. Roberts to have done this shows that he had confidence that his action would be rightly construed, and I venture to say that by a great majority of men it would be. Mr. Dodds strikes a lower ncte than I am sure is usual with him when he infers that this was any part of a tacit admission that Mr. Roberts by this, thought his previous position untenable. Let Mr. Dodds as freely admit (without sacrificing any of his opinions) that other men may honestly differ from him, and make the same effort to credit them with honourable intent —and the difficulty is solved. In effect he has done this to a ,great extent, for how could he attend any rneetings, the known purpose of which was to secure Mr. Roberts' return to public life, unless he knew that Mr. Roberts was a proper rnan for the positions his prominence would entitle him to in public life when returned ? Let him do this a little more csurteously and using a nicer selection of terms than in his recent letters, and Warrington will be spared the existence of the animus of two prominent citizens and secure the united service of two very able men. -Yours faithfully, ARTHUR E. BECK.

Earleswood Hou3e, near Birmingham. June 11th, 1003. The Technical School.

am quite in agreement with the writers of the previous articles, re inefficiency of the Technical School Laboratory. lam also in favour of considering the efficiency rather than the enlargement of the present building, which in my opiuicn is quite unnecassary. The quantity and quality of the apparatus for the upper stages is absolutely inadequate, without considering the fact that several of the more expensive, but still necessary pieces of apparatus have not as yet been pro- cured. I think it extremely unfair, that seeing we have to go through exactly the same examinations as the more modern schools (i.e. Manchester), and also pay the Same fee, we have not the same facilities for preparing. What is wanted is a separate laboratory for the upper stages with efficient and modern apparatus, and to be entirely sepanite from secondary students and first stage e vecin,g students. —I remain yours truly, ADSL7M.

Hooliganism Rampant

Sir.—Serne rears ago I was in a town not twenty miles from Warrington when I heard two persons debating. One asserted that several in that locality would not be able to say where God lived. To settle the matter, I walked up to some women and asked could they inform me where God lived ? Imagine my sur- prise when I was directed to inquire of Jack D., who had always lived in that locality and was acquainted with all %be lived thereabouts. On inquiring horn him he asked," Is he a pit-man or a bank-man ? "and after some further consideration he said, " I've never heard of him." This incident bas been recalled rather ! forcibly to my mind these last few weeks by what I've seen and heard in the Warrington streets ; and one is often tempted to ask: Where are the police ? But possibly they are not altogether to blame. Is there no bye-law in the town for suppressing foul language or the breaking of bottles in our thoroughfares ?! Both practices are far too prevalent in our streets. I Even in Sankey-street the use of senseless adjec- tives is most disgusting and is anything but a credit to the town. I walked round what is popularly known as Sixpenny at 1.45 p.rn.,the other day and the way several youths on their way to the Grammar School were hustled, jostled and insalted made my blood boil but there was no guardian of the peace in sight anywhere. The offenders were youths in greasy garments on their way to the Dalton Bank Wireworks, which place I passed at 6.30 the other morning. There I saw several young men I

who'presnmably had been working the night-turn but then they were making the place hideous with their , shouts at one another and kicking each ether's tin cans, and making very rude remarks about everyone that passed by quietly. I went along B. street to D. lane at 2 p.m., the other day and saw a Ind (certainly not 12 years of age) bringing an old pickle bottle with beer in it from an adjacent public-house. This is hardly . lin conformity with the law is it ? But as in other matters this seems to be observed more in its breaking ; than keeping.—Yours &c., OBSERVER.

LYMM MAY QUEEN.

A Successful Festival.

The old-world peacefulness of Lynam gave way to animation and gaiety on Thursday when thousands of people from Warrington, Manchester, and all parts of Cheshire invaded the quaint village to witness the May Queen Festival. Lynam was looking at its best and the narrow cobbled streets and winding lanes, rich with foliage and bloom, made a charming setting for pretty pageantry. Within the shadow of the ancient cross a huge concourse gathered to see the marshalling of the procession. These waiting there heard in the early afternoon tha approaching pomp of bands, and saw the Queen's retinue file past merrily. There was plenty of sun- shine when the procession set out, although the revels of the newly-crowned Queen later were rather dulled by clouds. One felt that the child-actors themselves had the best of things as they went along raised high over elder heads, full of the pride of being seen. The 1 characters were tho sound traditional ones, such as ;Grace Darling in a harry pretending to be a boat ; • the old woman with a shoeful of beaming boys and ; girls ; harvesting girls all cream and roses, with a dainty stock of bay and brandished rakes. Then 1 there were military heroes, characters famous in !- history and groups of merry dancers. Perhaps the villagers most enjoyed those guileless humorists the comic cyclists. Following the band of the " Inde- fatigable" training, ship, came the Rose Queen (Hiss ! Ethel Hullineaux,daughter of Acting-Sergeant and Hrs. , Mullineaux)and her attendants, and last,of all preceded by the bearers of the regalia andXx mails of honour, rode the May Queen (Miss Ada Jones, daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Jones, Higher-lane). " Her Majesty " 1 comported herself with queenly dignity and gracefully acknowledged the plaudits of loyal "subjects." Altogether there were six hundred children in the procession which was-considered to ho the best since the festival was organized twenty years ago. 1 The procession " beat the bounds "of the Lynam lanes and made its way finally to a field, where the ; Queen was crowned. From her high red throne she overlooked —beyond the arena of the revels —the wide ! ! Cheshire plain, seen between grand chestnuts and limes. The crowning was done slowly and with ! reverence, and the sceptre presented with portentious ' solemnity by a boy in purple velvet. The Queen was ! crowned by Mrs. F. W. Ashton, president of the Ladies' Committee. The costumed children, sitting in a wide semi-circle below the throne, made a beautiful picture. After the crowning followed the sports, which opened with the Maypole dance, • danced to good old English tunes by a group !of children who did not muddle a single ribbon. • Other pleasing features were a hoop drill, naval 1 display, fairy dance, gymnastic display, flower dance, 1 Welsh dance, and tambourine dance, the children ; acquitting themselves admirably and evidencing very !!! careful training. In the evening the revels were ; resumed and the Lymm Subscription Band and the band of the "Indefatigable" played for dancing. The arrangements were well carried out by a large and representative committee, the chief officials being: Chairann, Mr. Edwin Brown; vice-chairman, Mr. J. B. D. Smith ; hon. treasurer, Mr. F. A. Morris; lion. ! secretaries, Messrs. G. H. Howard and F. W. Ashton; MC., Mr. T. Nash; Ladies' Committee : President, I Mrs. F. W. Ashton ; lion. secretary, Miss H. Brundrett.

MURDERERS AT LARGE.

j Hardly a week passes bat some murder is committed and the guilty person escapes. The saying that ! " murckr will out " does not hold nowadays. The con- trary is, indeed, more nearly the case. Notwithstanding i all the brains at Scotland Yard, a murderer more often than not gets the better of the police. The following are the official figures relating to , murders in England and Wales during the ten years , ending December, 1833 :—.llnrders reported, 1.760 ; no trace of criminal, 1,091 ; persons tried, 672 ; acquitted, 1131 : found insane, 142 ; convicted and sentenced, 200. I This proportion is about normal. Between BD and ; 1900, inclusive, the police reported 2,610 murders, of which 1,472 baffled the efforts of Scotland Yard to solve, while of the 1,133 suspected persons sent to trial, upwards of one-fourth were acquitted. The figures for the last two years for which there are complete returns are as follows : • 1905. 1903, I Murders reported I No trace of criminal

NATIONAL FiIEE CdURCEI COUNOIL. MEETING OF GENERAL CONINIITrEE.

! The summer meeting of the General Committee of ' the National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches was held at the Memorial Hall. Dr. David Brook, • president of the Council. occupied the chair. The Secretary, Rev. Thomas Law, read a communica- tion from the English and Welsh Councils of Swansea invitinr, the Annual Council to meet in that town in March, 1909.-0 u the motion of the Rev. J. G. Green- hough, M.A„ seconded by Rev. J. Hirst Hollowell, it was decided to accept this invitation. A report was presented on the arrangement made for holding the National Welsh Convention at Bridgend from Juno 20th to July 2ad, 1038, when representatives are expected to be present from the whole of Wales. Dr. Brook will preside over the Convention, and the following are amongst those who have promised to be present and take part :—Mr. William Brace, M.P., Sir Samuel T. Evans, ICC., M.P., Mrs. D. Thornas Law stated that arrangements are I now complete for Gipsy Smith's tour in America. Gipsy Smith will leave England in August, and will I hold great United Missions in Baltimore, Washington, Cleaveland, New York, Pittsburg, St. Louis, Kansas ' City, Denver, St. Paul, Minneapolis and Toronto. All the arrangements for these missions have been made at the National Council Headquarters in London. The Social Questions Committee report the adoption of the suggestions respecting the Social Work of the I Churches. The first Social Questions Conference ilarranged will be held on July 13th, to deal with the , ISinall Holdings Act. Mr. Currie Grant, M.P., wilt introduce the subject. Mr. Law reported on the Rev. F. B. Meyer's tour in South Africa. Mr. Meyer's mission has opened most prosperously at Cape Town. At the meeting in the Town Hall, 3,000 people were. present, arid hundreds were turned away unable to get m. At Kimberley the same experience was repeated; Mr. Meyer is winning all hearts.

The Committee also considered the question of the organisation of Lay Preachers.

THE FUNERAL.

The interment of the remains of the late Mr. Andrew Jennings took place at Warrington Cemetery on Wednesday afternoon. The widespread feelings of regret and pain of the townspeople at the sad end of one who bore such an unblemished character, and whose life had been devoted, in tenderness and affection towards others, found vent in the many thousands who lined the route to the Cernetery, and the immense crowd that gathered round his grave. The coffin was accom- p Iniecl from the residence to Buckley-street Chapel, Winwick-road, by 53 agents of the National Union Life Assurance Branch, including all Royal London agents. Mr. Jennings was a respected member of the Union, having been an agent for the Royal London for 27 years. A service was conducted in the Chapel by the Rev. J. F. Matthews, who spoke of the deceased as a noble example of Christian service, having attended God's House regularly for -11 years, and serving nearly 30 years as a Sunday school teacher. He had been superintendent for 12 years, and continued in that office up to his death, being beloved by every scholar. One of his last acts was to go on June Ist to make final arrangements for a field and the lurries for Walking Day. His two favourite hymns were sung by the crowded con- gregation, and the Dead March in " Saul " was rendered inapressively by the organist (Miss Plinston). The cortege restarted, and was led by 300 school children and 200 adults and the agents ; then followed the hearse and seven coaches. The Rev. J. F. Matthews conducted a very impressive ceremony at the grave. Great sympathy was evinced towards the widow and daughters.. The blow which has fallen upon them is keenly felt. A large number of wreaths were sent by widow and family, relatives and friends, Royal London agents, National Union agents and others.

H. WHITHANI.

INSURANCE AGENT'S SAD END. -- Pathetic Letter Read at inquest.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The entertainment at the Palace and Hippodrome this week reaches a high standard and has afforded delight to large audituces. Bella and Bijou are two of the best society entertainers who have ever appeared in Warrington. Their turns bear the stamp of origin- ality and up-t i-dateness, and while they are on the stage merriment holds sway. Two of their latest successes are " The Spanish dancer" and "The Suffragette," the latter proving especially popular. The characters are a suffragette of a very advanced type who enforces her ideas in the household, and a husband who has been reduced to a state of abject servitude. In the end, however, the long-suffering worm turns and the authority of man is once more vindicated. The Schiavonis troupe of comedy acrobats are experts in their p irticular line of business and their daring performances almost take one's breath away. One member of the troupe, Miss Ella, accomplishes the unique feat of catching one gentleman on each hand, whilst they are throwing somersaults from the ground. Takio, the Japanese inimic,gives remarkably clever imitations of a great variety of sounds, ranging from a grinding machine to a cat and dog fight. Billie Burford, smart comedienne, scores a distinct success, her song "In a cheap excursion train " quite taking the fancy of the audience. Flo Burnett is an accomplished vocalist, possessing a voice of fine range and power, and her interpretation of " Come back to Erin" is deservedly applauded. Stuart and Evans, comedians and dancers, provide an amusing turn, and Phern and Phern, musical speciality artistes, demonstrate their skill on a number of novel instru- ments. Raymond's bio-tableaux shows a laughable set of pictures entitled " A. crazy cart wheel."

===== 0002601_19080613_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
NEXT WEEK'S PR-OC;RAIINIE.

The management have secured many new and inter- esting features for next week's. programme. The " bill " is headed by Leslie Brothers, eccentric musical comedians, who will appear in an original act, " Music Mad." Chanti, the modern Proteus, will give a marvellous and mysterious quick change act, described as the last word in novelties.Chanti will pay £l,OOO to anyone who can prove that he has any assistance xvbatever during his performance. The other artistes will be: Rose Sartella,refined American comedienne ; Tennis and Laune, in their vocal scena " Chapters from Life ;" Charles Resti, comedian and conjuror ; Zeta Gray and Company in their musical comedy act " The Lancashire Lass ; " Kitty Clinton, dainty and versatile comedienne ; Ruhart, in a rapid succession of miginal productions and novel burlesque on vaudeville.

MESSRS. F. H. AYRES LAWN TENNIS RACKETS.

This is just about the time when one discovers that last year's tennis racket is not calculated to give com- plete satisfaction throughout the season, and the enthusiast naturally casts about for a new " weapon." We should like to draw attention to the " Wilding " racket, manufactured by the famous firm of F. FL Ayres, Limited, ill, Aldersgate-street, London E.C. This racket was made to the specifications of one of the foremost exponents of lawn tennis, Mr. A. F. Wilding. It is a handsome article, designed to give the maximum amount of power with the minimum of timber. We never saw a frame so slender and yet so tough. This racket is specially strengthened by insertions in the frame at the shoulders, at the same time adding to the appearance, as no surgical or gut binding is required. The finest materials are combined with the most skilled workmanship. It has been used and recommended by many of the principal lawn tennis players in England and on the Continent. The following events have been won by Mr. A. F. Wilding. with the -" Wilding" racket :—Cannes, singles and doubles ; Barcelona, singles and doubles ; French covered courts, singles end &Ales ; Qtteen's Club covered courts, mixed doubles ; Wiesbaden, singles, doubles and mixed doubles, also Wiesbaden champion- ship ; Reading, singles, doubles and mixed doubles ; Sheffield, singles and doubles ; Leicester, mixed doubles': All England championships, mixed doubles ; Prague, singles, also Austrian championship; Epson, single and doubles ' • Shrewsbury, singles ; Homburg, singles, doubles and mixed doubles; Baden Baden, singles and mixed doubles ; Eastbourne, singles end doubles.

CHRISTIAN ENDEAVOUR.

THE 18th CONVENTION AT NOTTINGHAM. -- Special Report.

NOTTINGITAIf, TUESDAY. Thirteen hundred delegates from all parts of the United Kingdom were this week in the go-ahead, clean, green, city of Nottingham. There was fair weather, crowded meetings, very hearty singing, intent and responsive listeners._

fast night Mr. George Nicholls, M.P., in a fiery address on " C.E. in relation to the Labour Problem," said, whenever he spoke, it was generally along the line of this topic. He was inclined to think people might say" Well Nicholls is fiddling again on the old string." I think we are all agreed that these great social—l think I may call them Society—problems can no longer be ignored. We are agreed on that, and I want also to emphasise that this Christian or Christ's-man movement known as Christian Endeavour claims and holds a front rank position, not less than others, as a force for better- ment in the social as well as the spiritual life, in this and other lands, we stand for a reel risen Christ. I want to eraphasise that we stand for a real risen Christ in a living Church, for God and humanity. No true patriot to-day will venture to ask me, why lam I ray brother's keeper? Every student of modera land Social movements must have recognised the grow- ing need for greater endeavour on the part of the I Christian.Church,to strengthen the relationship between Ithe Christian and Social or Labour Problem. We must never allow any parson or section to suggest a separa- tion. Labour has in recent years become a great factor in public life, local, municipal or parliamentary. The man in the street is in many cases antagonistic to the ante in the Church. This ought not to be and need not be. Let me add we are fee to face, in many quarters, with varied and determined attempts c mstently made to alienate the working classes from tho churches, and in some nieces the Church has been driven into an i • attitude of defence both on the platform and in the pulpit. this I would suggest should never be necessary. Our work is to introduce Christ, not to defend the Church. We de not defend our homitals, bacataae they are stately buildings, costly and elaborate, or beeause they have attached to their foundation, some illustrious name. Because they are healing institutions, this is why we support them. So with the Church, my friends, it must be more than a stately building, with a tower as a landmark in the district, with a marble pulpit or a classic preacher. It must be to my mind what the upper Room at Jerusalem was, and what many a cottage and barn in our country districts has been since,—a soul saving institution where the souls of men and women are fired and filled with the Christian spirit. The Church,—aud I do not think anybody doubts it—has often been a mere hermitage. A holiday resort for a few pious souls ; when it ought to have been a generating station, sending a current of poamr—(lond applause)—a moving force into every house and social institution within its reach. I believe this to ha the function of the Church, end wherever you find her living up to her privileges, there you will find the working men will gather,—and they do—and the man in the street becomes the Man in the Church. Spiritual poweramd that alone ray friends, is needed both to win men and to hold the men you win. The greatness of any nation depends upon the integrity of purpose, and the courage and character of its citizens and therefore, if any man disgraccs himself he disgraces his country. That is the ideal. Hence if the goal before us be the uplifting of our race, this Can only be reached when man and man co-operate the individual being knit in society. We cannot have a regenerated society only through the channel of what we call the regenerate individuals, I believe in having Christians to take a foremost piece in municipal and local and Parliamentary life. I am in favour of establishing a good Samaritan Society on the Jericho Road, out of public funds, to care for those who .have been robbed and left on the road, and I would also fire out every robber from his cave along tho Jericho Road, and I make the Jerusaletn Council responsible for the firing out, and I would favour fighting every seat for the Council to see to it that the Christ followers are in the majority. I have great faith, as you will know, in the power of Parliament, and believe that we need many more good Acts placed on the Statute Book, and I am ready to de all I Can to help that on. But lam not going to sit down without saving that this high ideal embodied in an Act of Parliament, however well intentioned it may be, will never be reached except the moral sense of the community is also raised. What have you got to appeal to in any person but his moral sense ? What can you appeal to in the community but the moral sense of the community, and at best an Act of Parliament maker it easier to do right and herder to do wrong. Yes, but so long as the people are bent upon doing wrong, wrong will be dons in spite of your Acts of Parliament. I grant you may limit it somewhat, but you never save society until you save the individual, and I for ,one do not believe that you have done all that you can for a man when you change his residence from the back street to the front,although I would have every man more to the front. Education has dons much to better the towns, garden cities are doing more. The improved conditions in the workshope and employment at a living wage without loss of inde- pendeuce, these have been made a mighty machine for the social and moral uplifting of our race, but these alone fail, and as you must seek the salvation of the whale marl and every man, you have ' done little until yon have saved the man's spirit and soul and body. Set him right and straight within and without and you have accomplished something. As Meyer says" Yon must have the mainspring right as well as the dial." Now there are Some people who think that if you talk about the spiritual life it is a sign of weakness and that the advocate of social reform is the only strong man. It is better to be both of them and I want in this my last word to say, my fellow Endeavourers, that lam only an ordinary man with an ordinary vision, but I have clearly discerned after 21 years in close touch as a Christian with all sorts of society, On the farm, on the r ,ad, as a tramp, in the stone-pit, in the pulpit, oa the platform, the market,place and in Parliament, F am gain; to sty, as an ordinary mar I have clea,ly discerned three mighty. moving elements or forces in our national life—where- ever you ~r) you Had them—the social, the moral, the spiritual f'orme, but the greatest of these three as the spiritual and I look forward to and hope to help for- ward a Christianised and spiritualised democracy— England's one, and I believe, her only hope. This is my ideal. This is also my massage. There were several meetings preliminary to the Convention Session, viz., and principally Saturday: Mayor's Luncheon to Members of the National Execa- tive Council; Junior Rally, exercise by the Junior Endeavourers called "The Endeavour Bicycle"; Welcome Meeting in Castle Gate Church ; Praise Service, "The Blind Maid of Bethany," a sacred cantata by Frederick A. Jackson and Carey Bonner. The Convention Sermon, preached on Whit Sunday morning by the Rev. John Thomas, M.A. of Liverpool, in which he urged that "Love is the last word in character," was a truly spiritual effort and a fine sermon. An afternoon mass meeting for men, in the Tabernacle, was a fine sight. At the open-air Evangelistic Service on the Forest, at 8.15 p.m., about five thousand people gathered. ' At the Convention Session, Monday morning, Principal Bowser, 8.A., spoke on " Bible Study," an address richly stocked with the best possible advice to Bible students. Other addresses were given on the Christian Endeavour Pledge, the parts " I will pray," "I will read the Bible," "I will take some part." being dealt with.

At the Annual Meeting on Whit Monday afternoon, the Rev. E. Abbott was installed as the new President. He said " there was a very signiflcint item in the ex-President's report, viz., that he had travelled 20,090 miles. There is a lady in the gallery that will be greatly concerned about that.. But to get beyond this little pleasantry, this is the great opportunity of my life—a limited opportunity. This commission is given to me on the distinct understanding that I return it at the end of twelve months, and retire to oblivion at the back of the Presidential chair."

Monday evening's Meeting in the Victoria Hall was powerfully inspiring with the Rev. 3. D. Lamont of Belfast In the chair, and such !speakers as Mr. George Nicholls, M.P., Rev. Canon flicks of Manchester and Mrs. Higgs of Oldham, who spoke respectively ou Christian Endeavour in relation to the three problems. the Labour Problem, the Drink Problem and the Housing Problem. On Tuesday morning Endeavourers considered their responsibilities "To the Children," "To the Non- churchgoing," and "To the Regions Beyond." In the afternoon denominational rallies were held.

The closing meetings in the evening wore intended as Consecration Services, in preparation for the service which awaits the Endeavourers on their return to their homes and churches, throughout Great Britain. Many delegates were domiciled in "Hostels "—schoolrooms, turned into hotels—which although not quite as sweet as home, offered compensations in the way of good fellowship and novelty. Some of the beds and pillows in the hostels were of straw. A wit who heard of this suggested that perhaps the delegates might also be fed on turnips. Any little hardship concerning sleep, was however, made up for, by the excellence and abundance of food supplied by the Hospitality Committee workers. The Warrington and District Christian Endeavour Union has arranged for a" Convention Echo Meeting " to take place on SAturd,ty evening next the 20ch inst., in Providence Independent Methodist Chapel, Stockton Heath. The meeting will be preceded by a ramble, and by a tea in the above chapel.

Towards raising a Primitive Methodist Thanksgiving of £250,000 the Liverpeol district has given a pledge to raise at least £16,000. Towards this amount local circuits have voluntarily assessed themselves for £13,226, and 22 have yet to make their returns. The Manchester district assessed itself for £25.000 and the circuit promises exceed £30,000 apd more is expected. Throughout the denomination satisfactory progress is being made. Two-tbirds of the stations have under- taken to raise £211,000. These figures give some justi- fication for the hope that when complete the Centenary Fund will reach a higher figure then the quarter of a million the Manchester Conference decided to aim at.

LORD NEWTON'S DAUGHTER MARRIED.

illasses of white lillies and tall palms formed the decoration at Sr. Peter's Church, Eaton-square, on Thursday afternoon, when the Hon. 'Attica Legh, daughter of Lord and Lady Newton, was married to Mr. John Egerton Warburton, of the Scots Guards, and of Arley Hall, two well-known families in Cheshire being thus united..

Th-e bride was given away by her father, and wore a lovely gown of white satin char:nen" made Empire style, and embroidered with silver. liar Brussels lace veil was arranged emr a chaplet of orange-blossom, and round her neck was a single row of pearrs, tho gift of lady Newton.. - . . .

Her.'eight bridesmaids made a picturesque group in white chiffon gowns over white satin, with touches of pale pink, and included the two younger sisters of the bride, the Misses Egerton Warburton, the Misses Villiers, Miss Egerton, and Lady Cicely Pierrepont. daughter of E trl and Countess Mauvers. Their white straw hats were trimmed with white tulle and adorned with one large pink rose, their bouquets being of pink roses, and each wore the bridegroom's gift—a ruby and chrysoprase brooch. After tho bridesmaids came some of the non-commissioned officers and men of the bride- groom's regiment, in the uniforms of the Scots Guards, who lined the aisle throughout the service. The Bishop of London officiated, assisted by the Rev. G. Williamson, of St. Peter's, and the Rev. C. Thomp- son, chaplain to Mr. Warburton, and the bridegroom was supported by a brother °liner, Mr. J. Wickham, as. best man.

The large congregation includad the Duchess of Buckingham and Chandos, wearing black and white and white striped voile ; the Earl of Dartrey, and his daughter, who was in white and purple ; and the Countess of Dnadonald, who br.mght her daughter. Viscount and Viscounte3s Lewisham were together. Lady Cynthia Colville was in blue chiffon, Lady Ampthill was in resada cloth ; Camatess Cro:nartie was a late arrival, wearing white and gold. The Hon. Mrs. Rowley, the bride's aunt, was in grey satin. After the wedding, Lady Newton, who wore a lovely gown of soft grey, with lilt to match, and carrying a bunch of mauve orchids, held a largo reception. The honeymoon is being spent at Billhill, Wolringbam, kindly lent by Sir Edward and Lady Ridley, the bride's uncle and aunt.

The bride received some very lovely jewellery. Lord Newton gave her a diamond tiara, and her brother and sisters a peridot and diamond aigrette. The bride- groom's gifts included a diamond and sapphire ring, and a ruby and diamond pendant. Mr. Egerton Warburton gave her a pearl and diamond necklace, and a large diamond pendant was the gift of several friends who joined together.

' ticket ?" he asked her abruptly. " Oh, no," she said mildly," I'm not going this time, but I wanted to know the particulars. My sister may be going in July." • * 1 To-day should be a red-letter day in the history of the women's suffrage movement. Twp great open-air demonstrations in London have been arranged, the Imore important being the march of the 10,000 women from the Embankment to the Albert Hall, organised by the National Union of Women's Suffrage Societies. These ladies represent the constitutional as opposed to the mil taut agitators, and their procession will furnish a striking object lesson in the spread of the movement among business and professional women. Marching six abreast will be medical and University studeats, typewriters, clerks, artists, musicians, writers, nurses, actresses, and many other classes of female workers carrying hundreds of specially painted banners, and accompanied by numerous bands. At the same time Trafalgar-square will be occupied by a demonstrati ,n got up by the Industrial and Trade Union Women Suffragists, and representative of four teen of the leading societies for the protection of female labour. Both of these gatherings, however, are likely to be eclipsed by the great assembly in Hyde Park to-day week, arranged by the militant suffragists, who are counting upon a crowd of a quarter of a million sympathisers. if the advocates of votes for women had from the first proceeded on these constitutional lines the movement would have held a warmer place in the sympathies of the general rublie than it does to-day.

PEDLAR FOX. Pedlar Fox was passing, 4 Looking very sly, "Pretty Mother Goose, Won't you uome and buy Ribbons for the goslings? Pay me with a smile; And if you will kindly Let me rest awhile." Mother Goose was wiser; , Thro' his guile she saw, "I love ribbons, but, sir, My children I love more." A POOR MUSICIAN. A poor old soldier; blind, and thinly el and bowed with age, used to play on a vlo.in every evening in the public gardens at Vica:r.a. Beside him sat his faithful dog. One evening he was sadly grieved. No one listened to his playing, and hot a single coin had been placed in his hat. Wearied out, and sad at heart, he sat (town on a stone, and, covering his face with his hands, wept. A gentleman who had noticed him sterped forth from the crowd. Going up to the old man, he put a coin in his hat, saying, "Lend ins your violin." He then tuned it carefully, and added, "While I play, you take the, money. Then he began to play so beautifully that a crowd of eager listeners soon gathered iound. Not copper, but silver also, was freely droll;es.l into the old soldier's hat. The'dog, whO held the hat, began to growl; it was so iffavy ha could not hold it. "Who is he?" every one began asking. He was one of the most famous violin players in the world, who was thus using his skill to help 3. poor old soldier. As soon as he had finished playing, he placed the violin in the poor man's lap, emptied the money into his bag, and before the old man could thank him he had gone. JOIIN W. LENDSAY. —4— A RAT'S FUNERAL. Some rats which lived on the banks of the River Meuse (in France) were daily fed by a little girl and her mother, and became so tame that at last they Caine regularly to the kitchen door for food. One a them was very old and blind, and could hardly walk. He was always accom- panied by a ypung rat, who took great care of him, and, on account ofiullis infirmity, he was a great favourite with the people of the house. One morning he and his 'companions were missed, and for two days nothing was seen of them. On the third day the rats were observed to leave their hole in a different way than usual. First caine two' of them, dragging the poor old rat by his tail. He was dead, and several others followed in a kind of proeessien. " Mother, what's a tonic?" asked Molly, when at tea the other day. "Something that makes you feel well when you have been ill." "Then is a Teutonic something that makes you feel too well?" said the girl, with an air of superior knowledge. , Mrs. Goldstein: "Ikey, They I Felix has svallered a penny." Crapnenhall Parish Council. This Council held its quarterly meeting in the National Schools on Thesday-evening,the chair (in the absence of Ur. J. C. Parr), being taken by Mr. A. Lister Kaye. The other members present were Messrs. G. Ashton, Lawrence Bolton H. T. Nicholls, W. Lomas, T. Ireland, T. Collins and C: H. Hoult (clerk). —The Committee of the fhll Council appointed to consider the position of the Thomas Johnson Charity reported as follows : —" After carefully considering the matter, and having again communicated with the Charity Commissioners, the Committee is of the opinion that the Parish Council have no legal right to take possession of the charity. It appears, however, that additional trustees can, with the consent of the present trustees, be appointed by the Parish Council and others concerned.—Mr. Bolton said the report amounted- to this—that they had no power. The Parish Council would be well advised to let the matter drop. (Hear, hear.)—The Chairman: And even if we became trustees we could not administer the charity. That is rather a strong point.—On the motion of Mr. Bolton, seconded by Mr. Nicholls, the report was adopted.
